ASK : "Do you understand French ? " Wag- staff : "I do when I speak it."

"THERE goes a man who keeps his word." "He does?" " Yes ; no one else will take it." QUILP : "That man is at the very top of his profession, I cau tell you." Steerforth : "What is he 1" Quilp : "He's a steeplejack." MISTRESS: "Did you manage to find the basket of eggs that was on the pantry floor, Kate ?" Servant "Oh, yes, mum—aisily. I shtepped in it."

BIBBS : "Who was the man you gave that half- crown to at the hotel this morning ?" Gibbs: "An old literary friend of mine; author of How to Get. Rich.' "

A.: "How do you know that Alaler has come in for a fortune ?" B.: "Why, formerly people always. said he was crazy, now they say he's

_ _ original." - Parrs: "Why does Wheeler always ride his bicycle in front of the doctor's house ? ' Watts: "He is a beginner, and wants medical attendance handy in case of an accident." No matter whether a man's life results in absolute failure or complete success, plenty of people who had predicted it can always be found. A GROWING Evri...—Mrs. Biggs; "You remem- ber that organ-grinder you gave a penny to a week ago if he would stop playing? Well, he came round to-day and wanted sevenpence." Biggs: "Great Soot! What for !" Mrs. Biggs: Ile said he hadn't been here for a week !"

HOUSEKEEPER: "Pretty specimen you are to ask for help! The dirt on you is an inch thick." Tramp: "Yes, mum, times are werry hard, mum, and funera4 expenses comes high. I'm leaving it on so when my time comes I won't need burying."

As old Dutch tavern-keeper, who had his third

wife, thus expresses his views of matrimony "Veil, you see, de first time I married for lofe— dat vas goot; den I marries for peauty—dat vas goot, too, about as goot as the first; but this time I marries for money, and dis is petter as poth." THE DOMESTIC SERVANT PROBLEM. —" Come over to -dinner to-night, old man?" "Won't to- morrow do ? " "No. We've just got a new cook, and she may not be with us to-morrow." NEVER SATISFIED.—Wife: "Thomas, I wish you would let me have £5." Husband. "All right, my dear; here's a LIO note for you." Wife: "Oh, thank you, Thomas; but you forgot to pay me the 3d. you borrowed of me the other day when you were short of change." THE HUSBAND (on his death-bed) : "My darling, when I am gone, how will you ever be able to pay the doctor's bills ? " The Wife: "Don't worry about that, dear. If the worst comes to the worst, I can marry the doctor, you know." _ _ _

_ A BUSINESS VIEW. Business Man: "By George, it's going to rain. Here, you! I'd like an umbrella. You'll sell me one of those cheap, won't you?" Honest Umbrella-Mender: "Dose umbrel not mine." Business Man: "I know. Thatie why I thought you'd sell them cheap." THIS STORY IS FIVE HUNDRED YEARS OLD.—A countryman sowing his ground, and two young fellows riding that way; one of them, with an insolent air: "Well, honest fellow," said he "it is your business to sow, but we reap the fruits of your labour." To which the countryman replied: It is very likely you may, for I am sowing hemp." AN INDEFINITE PHRASE. —" I am in receipt of a princely- income," said the suitor for Miss Cool. cash's hand. "Better state figures, young man,"

replied Mr. Cooolcash ; "the last prince who pro- posed for Miranda had to borrow his fare home when she refused him." "WELL, Giles, I'm very pleased to see that my course of sermons has been the means of bringing so many country folks to our church." "Aye, indeed, zur. They was just a-talking o' it now at the Dragon." And what did they say ?" "They said as 'ow we never knowed wot sin was -until you cum'd among us." PHILANTHROPY AT SMALL COST. Friend : "Say, old boy, how in the world did you, with your small income, get such a reputation as a great philanthropist?" Sharpp : "I announced far and wide that I would pay the railroad fares of all the unemployed who wished to go to work on farms." " Well?" "Well, total expense so far ten

cents. EXPENSIVE LUXURIES. --Goodfello : "See here, Gayboy, you are killing yourself here at your desk. No man can stand working night and day all the year round." Gayboy : "Can't help it. I must have money." Goodfello : " Money ! You talk of needing money.. Why, you own one of the finest yachts in New York waters." Gayboy : " Yes ; that's why I need money." CAPPED.—Apropos of boasters, Dean Hole tells a story of an acquaintance of his at Oxford who once wrote him a note beginning—" My Dear Countess," and then scratched out "countess." substituting "Hole." Not to be outdone, the witty Dean began his reply—" My Dear Queen," and then drew his pen through "Queen" and sub- stituted "Dick." HIS WAY.—" Was your husband good to you during your long illness?" inquired the kind lady who was making a charity call. "Oh, yes, indeed, ma'am !" replied the poor woman; "as good as could be. He was more like a friend than a husband." COWWIGGER : "You seemed rather amused over the idea of your wife wearing knickerbockers." Smith: "You would be amused yourself if you could have seen her when she tried to find some- thing in her work basket, and emptied the contents into her lap." A CORNITCOPIA.—" Where," said the land agent, addressing an audience of, possible purchasers— "where else on the face of the globe will you find in one place copper, tin, iron, cotton, hemp, grain, game "---- .And a voice from the crowd replied, In the pocket of my youngest son." LONGHEAD "Madam, I wish to draw your Attention to the sad case of a poor family. The father is too old to work, the mother is bedridden, and the five little children are crying for bread. To cap all, the poor creatures are about to be turned out into the street, unless some one pays their arrears of rent, amounting to £5." Mrs. . Kindheart : " How terrible ! Here is the money for the rent. By the way, may I ask who you Are ? " Longhead (as he disappears) : "Certainly, madam, I am the landlord.?' Too LATE.—Local Labour Leader (angrily) : "Our Meeting has been a fizzle, sir, because you broke your engagement to be here." National Labour Leader: "I expected to arrive here early, but the strike on the A.B.C. road delayed the trains. When I reached the hotel the waiters wero on strike. When I started for the hall the cabman were on strike, and when. I got a street- car the hour had come for the conductors and drivers to strike." " PurriNo ONE'S Foox IN IT."—A millionaire railway king has a brother who is hard of hearing, while he himself is remarkable as having a very prominent nose. Once this railway king dined at a friend's house,- where he sat between two young "ladies," who talked to him very loudly, rather to his annoyance, but he said nothing. Finally one of them shouted a commouplace remark, and then Said in an ordinary tone to t'he other "Did you ever see such a nese in all your life ? " "Par- 410n me, ladies," said the millionaire, "it is my brother who is deaf." A SIIQPPING DREAM.—She was a habitual shop- per, and she had beea working hard at a church fair. So it came to pass that she. fell asleep that night and dreamed a dream, and it was thus :—She vlalked in the midst of a great fair, larger and g rainier than 'she had ever seen, and every table WaB headed with handsome trifles and elaborate .e:.ehroideries. And everything was ticketed with name and price, boldly and clearly, so that she . read them as she passed. For, lo !. one was marked, "This cushion, worth two dollars, price , five du!lars.." And the next bore' the legend, •, This set of doyleys, worth three dollars, price se ei.ei dollars." And she torne'd to her guide and cried out: "What is this ? \Vieve,ani I? '' And he replied, in a clear and loud voice: "In the law' of Truth !" And she awoke and, 'behold! it was a dream.

All rights reserved. THINGS A WOMAN WANTS

TO KNOW.

RIBBON aria laoe are the two things most dear to woman's heart with regard to dress, and of these two she will surely have enough— if not a surfeit—during the coming season. Bah are largely used for our dresses, blouses, and also for our millinery, but it is of the two former I would speak to you now. All the smart dresses at the present time have either a jabot or stock of lace; anyhow a little very good lace is introduced into their composi- tion. _ _ _ _ _ _ _

For my first sketch this wefor lam gi- you al very smart blouse made oi che new ail... Aerial for this season—silk zephyr. This is a com- bination of silk and cotton, with decidedly the appearance of the former, in a delicate shade of pale blue, with a tiny self spot of silk on it.

A Sw

). Paper Pattern, 634.

To be had from PATTERN DM,WIEICT, 18, HENRIP-7& STILEET, LON , W.C.

The blouse is simply made, and has dependant from the collar a full frill of lace, over which bang two bows of ribbon on either side; one short, extending towards the upper part of the shoulder, and the other long, reaching almost to the edge of the lace. The collar-band is swathed with the material, and has a tab of lace on either side, with a big bow of ribbon standing out on each side at the back.

I think these bows of ribbon at the back of the neck are very becoming now that the hair is worn a good deal higher than formerly. With some people the hair grows naturally high, leaving the nape of the neck somewhat bare. To them a ribbon bow is quite a god send.

But I am digressing. Let me draw your attention to the sleeves of this blouse, which are made tiFht-fitting with gauged puffs; the upper part is gathered twice at the shoulder, and then the sleeves hang full to the elbow, beneath which again it is gauged singly. There are very many muslins shown this year, and this is a design which would look equally well in either muslin, thin silk, or crepe. Now that bicycling is more the rage than ever, and young girls, as well as their grown- up sisters, lative taken to the wheel, we must provide for them as well as for others. This week, therefore, I am giving a very good design for a girl's knickerbockers, to wear under a skirt when bicycling. I am greatly in favour of alpaca knicker- bockers for summer wear, and especially do I think them suitable for girls, for very often young people do not have their skirts lined,

Girl's Knickerbockers. (Age 10 to 14 years.) No. 3011. Pavr Pattfrn, 61c1. To be had from PATTERN DEPARTMENT, 18, HENRIETTA STREET, LONDON, W.C.

and in that case tweed knickers are very apt to stick to the skirt in an uncomfortable and unbecoming way. With alpaca this is not possible, and for this reason I strongly advise its use.

The knickerbockers here shown, you will notice, are made with a sloped band which buttons on either side. At the knee they are ,athered.into a band again, and can either be fastened with a buckle or elastic, according to fancy. For windy weather, girls who ,wear short skirts can still fasten them down just in the same Way as ladies do, only they should fast en them to the knee instead of to the ankle. About thirteen inches from the front of the skirt sew a piece of elastic about four inches long, to this fasten another piece of elastic the right size to go round the knee. On to one end of this sew a hook, and to the other end an eye. When the skirt is on, clip this band below the knee, and the skirt will bo kept perfectly in place without its appearance being in any way spoilt. Brown boots are so universally worn that you may be glad to know how to clean th.m. Sometimes we are unfortunate enough to pt them • stained when they are quite new: e.t any rate, after wearing them for some time, occasional dirty streaks come on them, viol these it is well to remove. I know of thne methods, either of which would, I think, pro7c efficacious - -

, Place the boots on "trees" and give them a washing, say _once every week or so, -vith saddle socp. Let them dry, and then pclish. Stains may also be removed by rubbing ,with methylated spirit. The third method is one which must be used with great care, but which will remove the most obstinate stains. Place a little powdered oxalic acid in a piece of flannel. tie it Up, and then dip it, into water and gently rub the surface of the leather with it. Do this occasionally, and you will find it beeps your boots in admirable preservation. Chops cooked in the ordinary way are not altogether suitable for an invalid, but if pre- pared according to the following recipe, they will be found not only very much more digestible, but exceedingly appetising. Select a: loin chop, bone it carefully, trim off all superfluous fat. and roll the long end round the fillet piece. Rub with oil, and grill over a clear- fire, taking care that the chop is not over-cooked.. Put a little butter on it, and serve with a small ornamental skewer run through it. Having considered the digesti- bility of the invalid's repast, the next care most ho 'to serve it daintily. See that :the 3-I,tpeQ and cutlery are faultless, and on se: vn"!.l* -the chop ,garnish it with sprigs of .71'cri peas, or whatever vegetable he

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY MAY 2, 1896 THE HIDDEN HAND;

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Prinee and Princess Henry of Prussia are to represent the German Emperor and Empress both at the Imperial coronation at Moscow and at Princess Maud's wedoing. It is very probable that Prince and Princess Henry will make a prolonged stay in this country,and reside during the yachting season at Albert Cottage in Osborne Park, which was occupied last year by the Duke and Duchess of Hesse, and the Crown Prince and Crown Princess of Roumania, and during the autumn at Birkhall House, near Banger.

Apropos of weddings, I ha‘e often marvelled, why, during the hot summer months, open landaus or victories are not substituted for the dull, stuffy-looking closed landaus and broughams into which bridesmaids and guests creep, with painful skirt-iqueezing and head stooping, and where their festive array is hidden from the interested gaze of lookers-on. It is a curious fact that while fashion has varied sevnral of the details of marriage ceremonie3, this particular one remains quite unaltered. In fine weather open carriages would have a pretty effect for bridesmaids and guests. Although they met at Naples the other day,the breach between the Emperor William and his brother Prince Henry, is by no means healed, and rxince and Princess Henry are going to Moscow- for the coronation only be- cause their presence has been particularly requested by the Czar and Czarina. There is no doubt the Kaiser would have put his brother aside had it been possible to do so.

The Hon. Mrs. Reginald F tzwilliam, the celebrated beauty, who lately had a very narrow escape from per- manent disfigurement, by accidentally setting fire to her hair, was a Miss Edith Lane-Fox. She is a cousin of the beautiful Countess of Powis and the Countess of Sarborough, who were also Lane-Foxes. The accident was due to the lady having indulged in the application of a certain petroleum wash and should serve as a warn- ing to any of us who are tempted to follow her example.

_ The list of titled ladies who work for the Press,grows longer every year. Three Duchesses,those of Cleveland, Sutherland and Bedford have at one time or another contributed articles to magazines. The Countess of Cork Is generally engaged upon some journalistic work, and Lady Constance Howard is equally industrious, employ- ing herself especially in sporting subjects, while Lady Greville and Lady Colin ,Campbell are in receipt of a steady income from several papers to which they are regular contributors. Mrs. Madeline Lucette Ryley, the authoress of" Jed. bury Junior" now being performed at Terry's Theaure, London. has written no less than twenty-two plays, and four rultfc operas within thelast six years. She prefers serious pieces,and positively revels in character-drawing. Plot, she says is quite a secondary consideration, and all that a dramatist requires to do is to hit upon a little group of congenial characters and then leave them to work out their own story. Mrs. Ityley is at present en- gaged upon plays especially cemmanded by Mr. E. H. Bothern and Mr. John Drew. The women of Cicinnati, although they have lost their hats, have evidently kept their heads, as it seems they are preparing a counter-move designed to strike terror into the hearts of the bold men who have passed a law vetoing high hats in theatres. The Women's Bill is framed with the intention of getting rid of the annoyance caused by men leaving their seats during the performance, or in the intervals, with the distress and discomfort which this inevitably causes those parties whom seat leavers have to crush. Now there is very little doubt they have something to com- plain of in this particular direction, but surely the best plan would be to insist upon the real remedy, vii., man- agers giving more room—an arrangement that would be acceptable to the majority of theatre goers in our country. In addition to a riotous carnival of colour on every hat, to be correct the shapes must be as rough and fantastic as possible. The Maw shapes I have seen this week may be called "rustic " for they resemble hats of hedge rows, ploughed fields, &c.' more than anything else in texture and irregularity. Some are simply made of a square or round of rough straw folded and twisted in any way that seemeth best to the officiating milliner. A passion for cutting out seems to have set in again. and the " tab" is to be seen on nearly all our garments- We have tabbed basques, tabbed skirts, and tabbed re vers. The rever as we have known it (simply the square cut edge of the coat turned back) had a certain grace about it,but in the tab raver it is difficult to find either reason or beauty. By the way coats are now being made either to hang open or to button up to the neck, so that we may either dispense with or exhibit a blouse at will. This is decidedly an item of news to be grateful for. The revival of the ruehe as a trimming should be wel- comed with delight by those of us who indulge in flame dressmaking, for nothing is more effective or so easily made. A lovely gown I met at an "At Home "last week had every skirt seam outlined with ruchings of glace silk,and its sleeve epaulettes adorned in the same manner. The pretty wearer hailed from Paris. A great deal has been said about home industries, but those of us who try to support them should first of all make sure that they are home industries. It appears that in Beleuim, a thriving trade is done in I Matt linen." The linen is not even woven out of the best _

Irish flax thread. for that is greatly run upon by silk manufactures. Now that an much of our flannel and linen is made of cotton, and so much of our silk is made of linen,it Seems difficult to know what one's garments are really composed of. What a hindering world this is ! For countless ages we have all gone to bed the wrong end upwards. The profound discovery has been made by a noted French physician who asserts that he has conclusively' proved that t, sleep in a bed prepared in the old faahinned way is to induce ailments of all kinds. The result of a com- plete reversal of things will he amazing. Insomcia will be Cured and nightmare a thing of the past. Sleep in the new position will be intellectual because more pro- found, and people inclined to nerve troubles or lung dis- ease will be vastly benefited thereby. After all this, it is clear that the sooner we put our feet instead of our heads upon our pillows the better it will be for us. Bicycle sports for women will be well to the fore this summer. At one of the London Clubs a gymkhana is already arranged to take place next month. There will be a Needle Race, In which competitors will ride a hun- dred yards, dismount thread a needle at a table and return to the winning post holding the nPedle in their hands. The Egg and Sonnn Race is to be over a course of 100 yards, the compettors riding with one hand, and holding the Peg and spoon in the other. Another item is riding one bicycle and leading another. There is to "e a bill-posting race also, in which competitors will ride fifty yards. nost a bill on a prepared howling, maunt, and ride 150 yards to the winning post. The more care- fully and neatly the hill is posted the more,likely will the candidate be to secure the prize. If the present mildness of weather continues, epicures must he prepared for short and sweet enjoyment of their favourite delicacies. Take plover's eggs for instance. Usually they are sent to market first from Holland and then by progressive stages from the North of England and Scotland. This year they have all come in with a rush and already are being hatched out in many places. Consequently they will soon be no more. Strawberries. too, are already marvellously cheap and will have but a brief innings. The same applies to asparagus. In short all enmeatibles, from dncklines down to spring cabbages, will be out of season, almost directly they are in. Now that eggs are so abundant and good, it seems more worth while trying some of the numerous dishes of which they are a good foundqtion. Here is one excel- lent recipe. Fry brown in butter one sliced anion, add half a pint of milk and put in six hard boiled e.es, cub in half. Stew over a slow fire for three or four minutes, stir into the pan two well whisked yolks, a dessert spoonful of chopped parsley, one ounce of grated cheese, a pinch of einnomon, pepper, salt, and stir over a slow fire for six or eight minutes. Squeeze a little lemon juice over and vrve hot.

CHAPTER XXXIV.

THE HIDDEN HOLLOW.

"On every side the aspect was the same, All ruined, desolate, forlorn, and savage, No hand or foot within the precinct came To rectify or ravage! • Here Echo never mocked the human tongue; Some weighty crime that Heaven could not pardon, A secret curse on that old Building hung And its deserted Garden I" —HOOD'S HAUNTED HOUSE.

Cap was a bit of a Don Quixote. The stirring Incidents of the last few months had spoiled her; the monotony of the last few weeks had bored her; and now she had just ridden out in quest of adven- tures. _ _ _

The Old Hidden House, with its mysterious traditions, its gloomy surroundings and its haunted reputation, had always possessed a powerful attrac- tion for one of Cap's adventurous spirit. To seek and gaze upon the sombre house, of which, and of whose inmates, such terrible stories had been told or hinted, had always teen a secret desire and pur- pose of Capitola. And now the presence there of a beautiful girl near her own age was the one last item that tipped the balance, making the temptation to ride thither outweigh every other consideration of duty, prud- ence and safety. And having once started on the adventure, Cap felt the attraction drawing her towards the frightful hollow of the Hidden House growing stronger w.th every step taken thitherward.

She reached the banks of the "Demon's Ran," and took the left hand road down the stream until she reached the left point of the Hone- Shoe Mountain, and then gong up around the point, bhe kept close under the back of the range until she had got immediately in the rear of the round bend of the "1-Licse Shoe," behind Hurricane Hall.

"Well," said Cap, as she drew rein here, and looked up at the lofty ascent of grey rocks that concealed Hurricane Hall, "to have had to come such a circuit around the outside of the Horse Shoe,' to find myself just at the back of our house, and no farthei from home than this There's as many doubles and twists in these mountains as there are in a lawyer's discourse! There! Gyp, you needn't turn back again and p:•11 at the bridle, to tell me there is a storm coming up and that you want to go home! I have no more respect for your opinion than I have for Mrs. Condiment's. Besides, you carry a damsel-errant in (Fest of adventures, Gyp! and so you must on, Gyp! you limit on!" said Capitola, forcibly pulling her head around, and then taking a survey of the downward path. It was a scene fascinating from its very excess of gloom and terror I It was a valley so deep and dark as to merit the name of the hollow, or hole, but for its great extent and its thick growth of forest, through which spectral-looking rocks gleamed, and moaning waters could be heard but not seen. "Now, somewhere in that thick forest, in the bottom of that vale, stands the house—well called the Hidden House, since not a chimney of it can be seed even from this commanding height I But I suppose this path that leads down into the valley may conduct me to the building! Come along, Gyp You needn't turn up ycur head and pull at the bit 1 You've got to go! I am bound this night to see the outside of the Hidden House, and the window of the haunted chamber at the very least," said Cap, throwing her eyes up defiantly towards the darkening sky, and putting whip to her unwill- ing horse. As the path wound down into the valley the woods were found deeper and thicker and darker. It occupied all Cap's faculties to push her way through the overhanging and interlacing branches of the trees. _ _ _

" G-ond gracious," she said, as she used her left arm roller vigorously to push aside the obstructions to her path, "one would think this were the en- chanted forest containing the castl,-, of the sleeping beauty, and I was the knight destined to deliver her! I'm sure it wouldn't have been more difficult."

"CAPITAL" CHAI.

The rumour to which the " Speaker " has given publicity, that Lord Salisbury is about to retire from the Premiership, that Mr. Balfour is to go to the House of Lords as Prime Minister, and that Mr. Chamberlain is to abandon the Colonial Office and become Leader of the House of Commons, does not receive the slightest credence in any well-informed quarter. Every possible reason points in precisely the opposite direction. If any change occurred at all it would not amount to more than Lord Salis- bury's resignation of the Foreign Secretaryship. Events during recent months have made the duties of that office very onerous, and the two functions of Prime Minister and Foreign Secretary are at present quite beyond the powers even of a man in the prime of life and in full health. Lord Salisbury is not now in the prime of life, and his health is not robust.

The Select Committee on the question of the un- employed is drawing near the close of its inquiry, and its report may be expected very soon after Whitsuntide. Only two or three witnesses remain to be examined, and the end of- the present week will probably see the evidence closed, when the committee will adjourn till after the holidays. Part ef the holiday pastime of the chairman, Mr. T. W. Russell, will be the framing- of the report, which is likely to give rise to considerable discussion. It is probable that the extreme party in the committee will prepare a minority report representative of their own views.

There is reason to believe that the. Venezuelan question was before the Cabinet at their meeting on Monday, and it is not at all likely,that Ministers separated without discussing President Kruger's answer to Mr. Chamberlain's invitation. I was formed that when the official correspondence is pub- lished it will be found that British diplomacy has suffered rather a signal defeat. At all events. Mr. Chamberlain's statement in the House has caused much depression in quarters specially inter- ested in Cape affairs. There is no doubt that the Colonial Secretary was misled as to the feeling of the Boer President in the matter. It is inconceiv-, able that he would have acted as he did if he had felt any serious doubt as to President Kruger's ac- ceptance of his invitation.

The attitude of the Irish landlords towards the Land Bill is known to be causing the Government some concern. If the Bill is not carried they will have to reckon with the Ul.ter tenant farmers. It would not have mattered much if the Nationalist members had displayed a hostile feeling, and the Bill bad been lost through their opposition But if the Bill has to be sacrificed owing to the hostility from the Ministerial benches Ministers will be placed in a very owkwarc. predicament. In the circumstances Mr. Balfour may,-after all, take the bull by the horns and insist on sending the Bill to a Grand Committee, as Mr. Courtney suggested.

Though the telegram which Mr. Chambet lain read from Pretoria, announcing sentence of death on the leaders of the Reform Committee, was listened to by the House of Commons in the profoundest silence, members no sooner reached the lobby than their tongues.were loosened, and the wildest and most reckless fabric of speculation immediately sprang into the air. War was openly prophe‘ied against the Transvaal if Ole sentence was carried out, and the belief was expressed that no Govern- ment would be allowed to hold office for a day that did not at once declare war. Now, at least three- fourths of the nation will be strenuously opposed to the throwing away of an enormous number of English lives and the sacrifice of an incalculable number of English millions of pounds to avenge the execution of men who have been parties to inflict- ing more harm to the empire than perhaps any four men have ever done in the whole course of our colonial history. Whatever the music halls may say and sing about Jameson—and even that cult is dying out—these Johannesburg gentlemen are not heroes; and their whole attitude towards Jameson and the amateur revolution in which they have made contemptible figures for themselves has secured for them unmitigated scorn. England will go the length of appealihg for mercy, but her Government will not be allowed to go the length of sending 40,000 men to South Africa to fight several bloody battles.

Arrangements are being made in the Rouse of Commons for a chess match between representa- tives of the Government and the Opplsition. The competitors will number eight on each side. It is proposed that each pair of players Shall play three games, and the side scoring the highest aggregate will be the winners. It is whispered that the best chess player in the House is to be found among the followers of Mr. John Redmoni, and that the next best iv a well-known Irish Unionist. The games will be played in the smoking-room, or elsewhere, as the players may find convenient.

Is Mr. Henniker Heaton about to add to his Post Office triumphs, and perhaps also to the perplexities of his party, by becoming a land reformer " Born a serf on the estate of an English peer "—so Mr. Heaton writes of the late Sir Henry Parkes. He is the first Tory Member of Parliament, so far as We know, to employ the appropriate word.

It may have been noticed that on Monday night, when Mr. Balfour's motion for taking the whole time of the House was under disoussion.Mr. Healy's voice was not heard, though both Mr. Dillon and Mr. John Redmond had a good deal to say on the subject. The silence of Mr. Healy was accounted for by his absence; and his absence has a moaning in it which shows how worthless were any pledges either by Mr. Dillon or Mr. Redmond that, the Land Bill would not be discussed at very great length. The fact is that Mr. flealY. has been buried for some time among law:books in the Dublin Law Library, to which he has transported himself for the purpose of studying the Irish Land Bill in the light of the legal decisions upon points bearing on the adminis- tration of the various Land Acts. 11,1 will therefore return to Westminster the most thoroughly- equipped debater in the House upon the various proposals of the Bill, and by sheer force of his knowledge and ability he will take the leadership of the Anti-Parnellites practically,if not nominally, out of Mr. Dillon's hands. But the fact that Mr. Healy has made this pilgrimage to the Dublin Law Library is a sufficient assurance that the committee stage of the Bill will neither be short nor easy, and it may lead to new developments before the session is quite over.

The feeling in regard to Mr. Chamberlain's recent action at the Colonial ()film is becoming more hos- tile every day among Conservatives, and a very animated debate is now certain to take place next Friday, or whenever the Colonial votes Come on There is every indication that Mr. Chamberlain will be severely criticised, not only by his friends below the gangway, but also by the leading members of the Radical party.

A gleam of liveliness was imparted to the Agri- cultural Rates Bill discussion on Wednesday by a raid made upon the landlords by Mr. Labouchere, who spoke with all the impartiality of one ,who, being opposed in talc to subventions in aid of local rates, is as strongly antagonistic to Sir Henry Fowler's amendment as to Mr. Chaplin's bill. Mr. Labouchere, maintaining that landlords are state tenants, the condition of their tenancy being the obligation to bear lozal burdens, was enlarging on their sole object being to exact rents, when Coleael Mellor interposed that he has plenty of land which pays no rent. "Will you give it to me ? " asked Mr. Labouchere. Co!onel Mellor declined the invitation. "All," said Mr. Labouchere, "because you want rent." The inisterialists gave evidence of the unpalatableness of Mr. Labouchere's home thrusts by indiscreetly, through Mr. Muntz, raising a point of order, objec- ting that references to the Chinese method of ap- plying the bastinado to those wile let their land go out of cultivation was not germane to the debate. This encouraged Mr. Labouchere great' y,as evidenc- ing the discomforture of his opponents. For the rest,the debate dragged its weary way along throughout the entire afternoon. Speakers were almost more plentiful than hearers, the groups who sought to attract Mr. Gully's eye at every opening Suggesting that the audience was chiefly composed of those less anxious to hear the views of others than to watch for opportunities to air their own,

THE EX A MINER, SATURDAY, MAY 2, 1896.

MARRIAGE OF MR. F. R. ROBERTS, OF

WARRINGTON.

The marriage took place at Sunderland last week, of Miss Daisy Ranken, daughter of Councillor D. Ranken, of Sunderland, to Mr. F. R. Roberts, M.A., L.L.8., solicitor, Warrington, and youngest son of Mr. Richard Roberts, of Bewsey- road. The wedding ceremony was performed at the Grange Congregational Church, Stinderland, and was witnessed by a number of friends of the bride and bridegroom. The church had been tastefully decorated for the occasion by the scholars and young ladies of the church. ' The bride was given away by her father,and was handsomely attired in a dress of ivory white broche, trimmed with white silk. She wore a hat of fancy white straw, trimmed with plumes, and carried an exquisite shaver bouquet composed of Marechal Neil roses. She was attenned by Miss Ranken and Miss Ethel Roberts (daughter of Coun- cillor Henry Roberts, J.P.), as bridesmaids. Their dresses were of yellow crepon with white silk trimmings, and they wore Leghorn hats, trimmed with white rib- bon and daffodils. They also carried bouquets of daffo- dils. Mr. F. Brockhill, of Newcastle, fulfilled the duties of best man. The officiating elergyman was the Rev. William Spensley. After the wedding ceremony the party proceeded to the house of the bride's father, at Azatia Terrace, where the wedding breakfast was served. Amongst the guests present were .Councillor and Mrs. Rinken,Councillor and Mrs.H.Roberts,J.P.and Miss Ethel Roberts, Mr. and Mrs. Charles Ranken, Mr. and Mrs. F. Ranken, Miss Ranken, Miss R. Ranken,Mrs. D. Ranken, Mr. Dave Ranken,and many others. Toe newly-married couple afterwards departed for Keswick where the honeymoon will be spent. l'he bride's travelling dress was a coat and skirt of heliatrope coloured cloth with hat to match. As will be seen from the extensive list of presents appended, many costly gifts were received.

NOTES AND NEWS.

General J.- übert, cf Transvaal fame, is a mild-man- nered, soft-voiced man, long past middle age. He was a plain, unpretending Br farmer until he came to the front in the war of 1830. He is a better general now ; his admirers say that he is a strategist worthy to compete with the best European generals.

The Duke of Norfolk has been seriously considering the desirability of utilising the cycle in order to expedite the delivery of telegrams. Of course the use of cycles would not be practical:le in certain districts,but it would in most of the chief industrial centres. Moreover, they could be utilised to reduce the cost of porterage—now a serious matter where, th,l distance exceeds three miles. The Postmaster General intends attaching cycliAts to a number of carefully selected offices. Of course this will be in the nature of an experiment,but if it answers, the scheme will probably be immediately extended.

I pavenr.c,nt ; .d ii Vienua, consists of granulated csrk mixed with miheral asphalte and other cohesive sub- stances,compressed into blocks of suitable size and form. Among the numerous advantages set forth in its behalf are cleanliness, noiselessness durability, elasticiy, free- dom from slipperiness,whether wet or iry,and moderate cost. Unlike wood, too, it is non-absorbent, and conse- quently inodorous. It presents the minimum resistance to traction, and, being elastic under passing loads, does away with the vibration caused by heavy teaming. The blocks are embedded in tar, and rest upon a concrete base six inches thick.

The Sultan of Morocco uses bicycles as instruments of torture for any of the ladies of his harem who have the misfortune to offend him. The unhappy odaliiques are compelled to mount machines and ride around a marked track in the palace gardens. Not knowing how to ride their repeated falls and other mishaps furnish the Sultan and his more favoured wives with endless amusement. When they have fallen 20 times—provided, of course, that they have not broken their necks in the meantime —the punishment is complete, and the bruised beauties are allowed to retire.

The report of the mining engineer of the Transvaal Government for the year 1895 is a very interesting document. During the twelve months 3.917.078 eons of quartz were mined on the Rand; and 3,601,697 were milled, producing 1,494,060 ounces of gold, valued at £5,383,540. The " saying " of gold from the tailings, Sze., brought in £2,309,470 more. On the other gold fields, such as Lyndenburg and DI Kaap,401,636 tons of quartz were milled, and the total value of the gold re- covered was £357,385 The grand total value for the whole Republic was £8,569,555. being an increase of £902,403 on 1894. There are 7,523 whites employed in the gold industry, and 54,17 natives.

Marshal Yamagata, who is now on the Continent on his way to attend the coronation of the Tsar, is not in very good health. He had a bad attack of influenza shortly before he left Japan,and his frienis insisted that he should be accompanied to Europe by a doctor. Ac- cording to the American papers the great Japanese general was therefore unable to accept the layish hospi- talities which were offered to him when he passed through New York ten days ago. What the Americans seemed to have remarked most about him was his modesty and his unmilitary appearance.

"The Coming of Rhodes" had a curious and amusing effect on the Portuguese authorities at Beira. So ex- cited were they by the approach of the" Colossus" that two gunboats and some 500 troops were sent to Beira, and the troops took up stations with Maxims and 7- pounders. On S. Patrick's Day there was great excite- m-nt. The hotel keepers raised green flags, cabbages, and all kinds of evergreens in honour of the occasion, and the Portuguese thought the hour had come for the dreaded revolt. A deputation to Colonel Machado suc- ceeded in convincing h,m of the harmless nature of the outburst of vegetables and evergreens. It is said that the formation of a local rifle club had something to do with the suspicions of what Mr. Rhodes might attempt. There are very few Englishmen at Beira, and a largo proportion are down with the fever.

"F. A.," obviously Mr. Frank Harris, essays to tells us in the "Saturday Review" of President Kruger as he is. The results may be brie& summed up. His personal honesty and courage are indisputable, he is a mighty hunter, the temper of his seventieth year is that of his early manhood, his moral courage is sa marked that it might be mistaken for obstinacy, he is impressionable and easily moved by those whom he trusts, in quite a peculiar way he twists Scriptural texts hr self-justifica- tion, he is a preacher of consideiable eminence. Finally, "he is worthy to be compared to Cromwell," and "if we must rank his ability loner, his inferiority may well be du sto the smallness of the field allotted to him by circumstances."

An extraordinary case has been investigated by the Liverpool police. An old man named Robert Shnlock, employed as a porter, after conveying a parcel to an address at New Ferry,crossed on his way back a field in which a donkey was grazing. The animal rushed at him, reared up and knocked him down with its fore legs.and afterwards worried him terribly about the head and body. The unfortunate man was afterwards found by a police officer and taken to the workhouse hospital, where he died.

It was reported to the Gloucester Board of Guardians on Tuesday that there had been during the last week 2,231 vaccinations and re-vaccinations. Dr.Sweeting,of the Local Government Board, addressing the Guardians, said nothing but vAccination would save Gloucester. Adults must judge for themselves whether they ought to seek its protection, but there were 3,000 unvaccinated &ildron in the city,who could not think for themselves, and the Guardians must without delay see after these by a system of organised vaccination, which required careful carrying out. The Board approved of the ap- pointment of ten additional vaccination officials. At Tuesday's meeting of the City Council it was an- nounced that the exnenditure caused by the epidemic would be spread over two years in special half-yearly rates of 9d. in the pound each.

Sir Donald Smith, the new High Commiesioner for Canada. is a native of Elginshire, but left Scotland at an early age to take a post in the Hudsom's Bay Company. He is a leading banker and railway potentate of Mont- real, and has played a considerable part in the commer- cial development of the Dominion.

It is stated that the joint property of the men con- demned to death at Pretoria awounts to £12,000.000 sterling.

AT THE F;)OT OF THE HILLS.

THE STORY OF A GIRL'S ESCAPE.

At the village of Troedvrhiw, in South Wales, lives, with her aunt, a Miss Rose Hancock. The village is very suitably named, for, as the Welsh derivation de- notes, it lies at "the foot of the hills," in the Taff Valley. From her plump, pleasant appearance one would hardly suppose that she had had a day's illness in her life. Yet, within a comparatively short time, she had passed thiough a most trying, and apparently a mortal: illness, which, indeed, made her friends despair that she would ever recover. Doctor after doctor was called in without success, a change of air and other means were tried, and all without success ; and when a lingering decline and death seemed the only prospect for her, a chance recommendation had such eminently gratifying results, that Miss Hancock was able to present the hardy-looking appearance in which the reporter found her. Miss Hancock, whose address is 36, Tyntaldwyn - terrace, Troedyrhiw, near Merthyr Tydvil, willingly granted an interview. She stated that she was twenty- one years old. About eighteen months previously she contracted a cold as the result of a slight chill, and it developed into symptoms of a most serious kind. It is Miss Hancock's opinion that she had dropsy. She was rendered extremely pale, weak, and feeble, and she was not fit to do the household work which she had pre- viously been doing. She states that she was very des- pondent, for she not only consulted local doctors, but was attended by medical men at Merthyr, and also went away for a change and yet after some months she made no improvement. Then it was, by one of those lucky chances which often have such a vital influence on human affairs, that she heard of the great benefits a neighbour had received from Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. On this recommendation Miss Hancock procured six boxes of the Pills, and after two boxes had been used she began to feel considerable benefit. She was speedily convalescent, eventually becoming as strong as ever, an able to do the work which had been too much for her just before. "Upon parents," says a well-known doctor. "rests a great responsibility at the time their daughters are budding into womanhood. If your daughter is pale, complains of w eakness, is tired out' upon the slightest exertion ; if she is troubled with headache, backache, pain in the side ; if her temper is fitful, and her appetite is poor, she is in a condition of extreme peril, a fit s bject for the development of that most dreaded of all diseases—consumption. If you notice any of these symptoms, lose no time in procuring Dr. illiams' Pink P:lls. They will assist the patient to de- velop properly and regularly; they will enrich the blood, and restore health's roses to the cheeks, bright eyes and a 1 ghtness of step will surely follow their use, and all dangers of consumption and premature death will be averted. %Vise and prudent mothers will insist upon their daughters taking Dr. Williams' Pink Pills upon the approach of the period of puberty, and thus avoid all chancess of disease or early decay." Dr. Williams' Pink Pills cure not only cases like the above, but all the many disorders which arise from an impoverished state of the blood and nerves, such as anremia,pale and sallow complexion, general muscul r weakness,loss of appetite, shortness of breath, pains in the back, nervous head- ache,early decay, all forms of female weakness,hysteria, 'paralysis, locomotor ataxy, sciatica, palpitations, low vitality, and wasting strength from any cause. These IPills are genuine only with the full name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, a.cd are sold by chemists and rby Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, 46, Holborn- viaduct, London, E.C., at 2s. 9d. a box,or six for 135.9 d. They are unrivalled as a tonic and strengthening Medi- cine for both sexes.

THE NEW GOVERNMENT BUILDINGS • AT WARRINGTON.

A glance at the plans for the new Government buildings at Warrington reveals the fact that the structure will be both handsome and commodious The plans have been prepared by Mr. H. Tanner, of Her Majesty's office of works, London. The build- ing will occupy the site adjoining the Gymnasium, and abutting upon Winmarleigb-st., and What is known as Palmyra-square,or Hanover-st. The block presents a picturesque appearance, and is de- signed after the Elizabethan style of architec- ture. The main entrance will be in Hanover. street, and there will also be two entrances from Winmarleigh-street. Th 9 structure will be three storeys high, above which rises a dome which is utilised in connection with the ventilation. The building will be faced with Ruabon best dressed red bricks, with mullions of a different material. The basement will be used for storage purposes and will also contain sanitary conveniences. Entering the ground floor by the main entrance a long wide pas- sage is reached. On this floor will be found a bankruptcy office, plaints office, registrar's private and business offices, and also a women's waiting room. By the Winmarleigh-street en- trances, access is gained to the offices in connection with the Inland Revenue department which will be found on the ground and first floors, The first flocr also contains a public waiting room. Close to, is a small room set apart for witnesses, another for members of the legal profession, and another for jurymen only. The registrar's court is fifty feet long, and running parallel with it is the judge court, 28ft. in length. The judge's private room adjoins it. The second floor will provide accom- modation for the caretaker. The floors to the up- per rooms will he fireproof. It is intended to ti.!;e Dennis, Ruabon or Burrnantoft's glazed bricks in connection with some of the inside work. The ground floors will be of wood blocks and tiles.

STRONG ENGLISH WORDS.

When a person says " I suffered excruciating pain," he expresses a fact in the strongest words afforded by the English language. The word " excruciating " comes from crux, a cross, and signifies an intensity of agony comparable only to that endured by one who undergoes the barbarous punishment of crucifixion. There are some diseases which, for a time,;cause pain of this acute and formidable nature. To find a relief for it, when

possible, is at once the impulse of humanity and the studious desire of science. Two brief examples may indicate what success is attending the effort to both comfort and cure cases of this kind.

" Nearly all my life," writes an intelligent woman, "I have borne the burden of what appeared to be incur- able illness. I always felt heavy, weary and tired. My appetitite was poor, and after eat:ng I had a cruel pain at my chest and between the shoulders. Frequently the pain was so intense th at I was impelled to loose my clothing and walk about the room My nerves were

disordered and impressible, and I was, consequently, easily disquieted. and ,upset. My sleep was habitually bad, and Lseemed non e the better for spending a night in bed. Eating but little my strength waned of neces- sity, and I came to be very weak. For a long time I got about feebly and with difficulty. "In August, 1887,1 had an attack of rheumatic gout, which gave me the most harrowing experience of my life. The complaint took its usual course and refused to yield to the ordinary treatment. Through the partial failure of the liver and kidneys dropsy set in and my legs and feet became puffed and swollen. I suffered excruciatinj pain and was confined to my bed for thir- teen weeks. Remedies of ovary description were tried, but to little purpose. "My brother, visiting me one day, said he had been cured from an attack of dropsy by a medicine called Mother Seigel's Syrup. I got a bottle from Mr. Hewitt, the chemist, in Seven Sisters'-road, and after taking it felt a trifle easier. I continued taking it, and soon the pain and swelling abated. I could eat without pain or inconvenience, and by a few weeks' further use of the Syrup I was not only free from any local all ment, but felt better than I ever did in my life before. Since then I have enj J yed continuous good health, taking a dose of Mother Sei;el's Syrup occasionally for some transient in.

disposition. Yon are at liberty to publish my letter. (Signed) (Mrs.) Elizabeth Rogers, 42, Plevna-road,South Totterham, London, September 13th, 1895." "In January,lB92,"writes another, " I had an attack of influenza, and was confined to my bad for eighteen weeks thereafter.Subsequectly I was very weok,and could get up no strength. Waat little food I forced down (having no appetite) gave me excruciating pais, so that I was afraid tm eat. I came to be exceedingly weak and

had frequent atttcks of dizziness. I was worn almost to a skeleton, and none thought I would recover.

"In June, 1892, M. Salith, a friend of ours, recom- mended me to try Mother Seigel's Syrup, which I at once procured of Mr. George Coombs, the chemist in Hueknall. After taking it for only one week I felt greatly benefited. I could eat better, and food agreed with me. Continuing with the Syrup I grew stronger, and soon felt even better than before I was attacked by the in fluenza. You are free to print this statement if you wish to do so. (Signed) (Mrs.) Ruth Halliday, 44, High- Street, Huckuall Torkard, Nottingham, March 19th, 1895."

Intense pain may or may not indicate urgent danger to life, but it is hard to bear, and very exhausting just the same. In cases :of rheumatic gout (Mrs. Rogers) the pain is caused by a pois onous acid in the tissues, origin ally produced by the decomposition of food in the stomach—indigesti on or dyspepsia. The same poison act;ng on the liver and kidneys creates the other symp- toms mentioned. In the case of Mrs. Halliday the ail- ment was dyspepsia, which in the first place invited in- fluenza, and then remained to torment her.

It is best and easiest to prevent pain by using Mother Seigel's Syrup immediately when the slightest illness

appears.

TWO BREACH OF PROMISE CASES.

Miss Julia Pickering, the daughter of a" bookmaker" at Hastings, was the plaintiff in an action for breach of promise of mar iage tried in London on Tuesday, the defendant being W. A. Law, an hotel proprietor. The engagement between the parties was contracted after a very short acquaintance, and ran the usual course. On the -10th of May the defendant was "yours till death," on July 10th the plaintiff was merely his" his dear Miss Pickering," and a short time afterwards he married a young widow. The plaintiff was "broken-hearted," and resorted in her extremity to a solicitor, who served the defendant with a writ. The jury awarded the plaintiff damages to the amount of £250. In the course of the proceedings it was mentioned that the plaintiff had" thou ht of going to Newmarket, as she was not well in health." The learned Judge said that was a peculiar place to go to for change of air, whereupon the plaintiff's counsel observed, Indifferently, that "many distinguished persons went to Newmarket." The learned Judge was Mr. Justice Hawkins, who is a great lover of horse racing. The bitter cry of a domestic servant at Cheadle who had been jilted by a farmer was also heard with much sympathy by a Manchester assize jury on Tuesday. The parties had shown none of the rash precipitancy which was disclosed in the Hastings case. In 1881 they were first introduced to each other, and in five years they were engaged. Ten years later they became disengaged, and defendant, after causing the plaintiff to waste ten or fifteen years upon "a mean-spirited fellow," posi- tively declining to fulfil his promise. Probably the fifteen years had seemed all too shorn' time to enable him to 'study the plaintiff's character and disposition. At all events,when pressed for what in higher circles is styled a declaration of policy, he pleaded that no time had been fixed for the wedding. The defendant did not venture to go into the "box," and the jury awarded the plaintiff the substantial sum of £350. Fifteen years would seem to be the normal duration of an engagement at Gatley, the defendant's brother having spent that length of time in "courting "the lady who now enjoys the happiness of being his wife.

WHAT WOULD THE CODFISH SAY?

If the codfish in the seas realised how great a bless- ing they are to mankind, they would not mind so much being caught, and dried and salted. At least, this is one way of looking at it. The oil alone that is made from their livers has probably saved more lives than anybody will ever realise. Most of its wonderful bene- fits are "born to blush unseen." Fortunately, some of them are not, and may thereby accomplish much good. Here is a case we are enabled to publish "In MayllB9o,l had a very severe attack of influenza, which left both lungs weak. Each winter since I have had bad attacks of bronchitis or inflammation,my lungs getting worse and worse, until I found myself in Janu- ary, 1895, quite unfit for my occupation,and had to give up my situation. The doctor pronounced it consump- tion, and ordered me to Clifton, where I was during February and March, without any benefit. About this time a friend sent me a bottle of Scott's Emulsion, asking me to try it. I did so, and thought I felt slightly better. The phlegm became less and the night sweats less frequent. In April I went into I Devonshire, my friends were thinking I had come to die,as I was so weak. Good nursing,and the regular use of Scott's Emnlsion,soon gave me strength,and I rapidly gained weight—over nine pounds in two months. I was ordered here for the winter,and I am glad to say that so far I have been entirely free from all colds, and am feel- ing stronger than I have for more than a year past. I hope with the regular use of Scott's Emulsion to be able to resume work in a month or two. For seven months I have taken no other medicine,although I am under the care of a doctor here. I told him what I was taking,and he said: "Continue taking it,as it agrees with you. It is a capital preparation,and to yeti is both medicine and food.' Thero are many case like mine. They cannot take the raw cod-liver oil,but if they tried Scott's Emul- sion,would find they could easily digest it-And if they be- gan taking it in time, woold gain strength, instead of gradually sinking away.—Yours very sincerely, (signed) EDMUND D. JORY, 3 Belvedere, Lincoln Avenue, Bournemouth, January 28th, 1896." It is interesting to know that Scott's Emulsion is a perfectly palatable preparation. It presents cod liver oil in an easy form for digestion and assimilation, and com- bines with it the hypophosphites of lime and soda in such a peculiar way that the potency of each is increased. In no other preparation can be found this ideal combina- tion, Red in no other preparation is there such perfect manipulation of the oil. This is why Scott's Emulsion is effective in eases where other forms of cod-liver oil have proved useless. A trial of Scott's Emulsion will convince you of its superlative merit. You can always distinguish the I genuine by the unique trade-mark of a man carrying a big fish on his back, which is on the wrapper of every genuine bottle. (16)

PRESENTATION AT THE Y.M.C.A.

ROOMS.

MR. STEWART'S DEPARTURE FOR CHICAGO.

There was an interesting gathering in the Lecture Room at the Y.M.C.A., last week, when the workers assembled to bid "God speed "to the late secretary, Mr. H. Stewart, :mho is proceeding to Chicago for special training. The proceedings tock the form of a social meeting. Mr. Wylie presided, and he was sup- ported on the platform lay hfr. A. H. Crosfield, Mr. Stuart, and Mr. Edmund Osborne (the new secretary of the Y. M. C. A.) In opening the preceelings, the CHAIRMAN said that was their last meethig with Mr. Stewart prior to his de- parture for Chicago, where he was going to enter an instituti ,n under the direction of Mr. Moody, the cele- brated evangelist, where instruction was given to Young Men's Christian Association secretaries. They regretted Mr. Stewart was abAut to leave them as they had spent manvpleasant hours together. Mr. Stewart had given a stimulus to many of the movements in connection with the Y.M.C.A. at Warrington. (Hear, hear.) But he was desirous of equipping himself for a higher and nobler work. His great desire was to work among young men, and he (Mr. Wyllie) was sure Mr. Stewart would carry with him their best wishes. They had also to welcome that night their new secretary (Mr. Osborne), who had boen connected with their institution for a great number of years. (Applause.)

Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD said he had come there that night hoping to participate in a social evening in which they intended to express in some degree their feelings towards Mr. Stewart, and to leave with him pleasant recollections of his association with the Warrington Young Men's Christian Association. He had intended to have joined in the proceedings to the extent of playing a tune or two on the piano. (Laughter.) He did not know whether it was fair to spring a speech upon him. (Laughter.) It was rather a delicate matter to pay com- pliments to a friend's face. It was, however, a great pleasure to him to be able to say a word about their good friend before he left for that vast country on the other side, of the ditch where he was going to take up his quarters for a time. He (Mr. Crosfield) had heard and read a great deal about Chicago and to judge from what one heard, there was ample oppor- tunity for a coming reformer in that part. (Laughter.) Still they were very sorry to lose their friend, Mr. Stewart. It had been said that Mr. Stewart's personality had counted for a great deal at that Y.M.C.A. They could not eliminate the personal equation, and it would be a bad job if they could. Mr. Stewart was a man of great activity and energy,w hi ch counted bra great deal. Everyone who had came into contact with him had felt the stimulus of his energy and his good example. After all, examole was a good thing. If example were better than precept he w.s quite sure that there was not a few of the members of the Association, and he hoped he was one of them, to whom Mr. Stewart had been able to preach a salutory sermon on many occasions. (Hear, hear.) He wished him God sp-ed, and success in the best sense of the word in everything he undertook. He (Mr. Crosfield) felt sure that Mr. Stewart would do justice to all the work that lay before him in what he trusted would be a very long life. (Applause.)

_ Short speeches were also made by the following gen- tlemen,who referred in felicitous terms to the services of Mr. Stewart to the Young Men's Christian Association:— The Rev. E. T. Reed, Messrs. James Hortonj. Bent, E. A. Sanders, Storey, Bayley, Fisher, and E. Osborne.

The CHAIRMAN said they were not going to let Mr. Stewart leave them wit Out some token of their apprer elation f his services. Rather unexpectedly he (the Chairman) had been asked G n behalf of the Bible Class and a few friends to present Mr. Stewart with a bible, which he would no doubt make his companion in future days. (Applause.)

Mr. STEMPLE; in a few appropriate words, expressed his pleasure at the gift. No more suitable present could have been selected. In referring to the friends he had made in connection with the Warrington Y.M.C.A., he said young fellows in Warrington did not know what they were missing in not being associated with it. It had been an education to him to come into contact with the men connected with the Warrington Young Men's Christian Association. There had been some zealous workers in connection with it long before he came to Warrington, and when he thought of the work which had been done in the past,he began to feel that there were many occa- sions upon which he might have done more. If he had the time to go over again he would try to do more than he had done. In conclusion, he thanked all connected with the ins' itution for the help and sympathy they had extended to him. (Applause.) After light refreshments had been handed round, a short musical programme was proceeded with. Mr. A. H.Crosfield very e:ficiently rendered several Scotch,Eng- lish,and Austrian airs,and a melody by Rubinstein. The vocalists were Miss Kent and Mr. A. Griffiths, and both sang in an excellent manner. Mr. H. M. Ashton, in his inimitable manner, contributed a reading entitled "The Training of Parents." The duties of accompanist during the evening were fulfilled by Mr. Hickman.

It may he added that Mr. Stewart sailed for Chicago z,i4 Montreal in the Allan Line R.M.S. Sardinian, from Liverpool.

SHIP CANAL.

THE TRAFFIC.

• During the fortnight ended April 24th, 333 seagoing cargo vessels, with an aggregate tonnage ot 93,407 net registered tons, arrived at or sailed from the various docks, wharves, ikc., in the Ship Canal. Of these,eighty vessels of 48,574 tons were engaged in the foreign trade, and 253 vessels of 44,833 tons were employed in coasting traffic. Compared wan the returns tor the preceding forteight, the traffic during the period under review shows an increase of eighty-two ships and 12,882 net registered tons.

HOW VIOLIN STRINGS ARE MADE.

The violin, it is said, is the only musical instrument that possesses a soul. But the quality of its harmonies depends as moch upon its strings as upon its body. The manufacture of these threads of catgut, from which a master of the art is able to draw such exvisite strains, an important industry. It - is, says s‘ Science Siftings,” is not carried on in this country, to any extent, at an events ;the finer qualities of strings are not made here, because English sheep have not the right sort of intes- tines: Catgut, contrary to the popular notion, is not derived from the domesticated tabby, but from lambs. A manufecturing centre for violin strings is the town of Markueukirchen, in Saxuny. The business there is a very old one, and is believed to have been started ori- ginally by immigrating Protestant Bohemians during or soon after the Thirty Years' War. The processes em- ployed have always been kept sccret, being banded down from father to son, generation after generation. However, the methods in a general way are sufficiently well known for purposes of descrinGon. In 1777,a union of string-makers was founded, and a law was passed forbidding any one not a member of this organisation from plying the craft, penalties of a fine and imprison- ment being establisned. The secret held most important was the composition of the lye mixture employed to bleach the strings. In old days. likewise, every maker of violins had his own peculiar varnish. He was perpetually seeking to dis- cover the philosopher's stone of his trade—namely, a varnish such as that used by Stradivarius, Guarneries, and Amati, which should impart to his instruments an imperishable beauty, while revealing the satin-like finish of the wood ben 2ath. But since the days of those celebrated and unequalled manufacturers of fiddles the production of such a varnish has been a lost art. The makers of strings at Markneukirchen, formerly glt their raw' material in Germany. But in 1840 the whole town was burned up, and one manufacturer went away in search of a livlihood, finally reaching Copen- hagen. Noticing large flocks of sheep in Denmark, he bought some of the guts and made strings of them, which were found to be of letter quality than the home brand. This discovery resulted in large importations of the article into Markt.eukirchen, and at about the same time the first strings were exported to this and •other countries.

_ The supply of material for the finest strings has at no time equalled the demand, and half the world has been ransaeked fer sheep-gut possessing the requisite qualities. In 1861 another enterprising citizen of Markneukirchca crossed Russia—at that time a venl:ure of no small peril —and 1 ought quantities of the intestines of the steppe sheep of that country. These were found tn be superior to anything previously obtained, awl to the present day manufacturers at Marknenkirchen get the bulk of their sheep-gut from Russia, amounting annually to about £40,000 worth. Other material is imported from Den- mark, Spain, Bulgaria, Turkey, Java and Palestine. Unfnrtunatelc. the Rtssian gut has deteriorated Since the filck owners have crossed the breed of the steppe sheep and the Spanish sheep for the sake of improving the wool.

The qualities desired in catgut for violin strings are a symmetric Ily cylindrical form combined with a trans- parent whiteness. Ordinarily the purchaser contracts with the breeder for all of his gut for at least one year. He has his own cleaning establishment annexed to the slaughter-house, and in person supervises the processes. Immediately after the animal is killed all crease and foreign matter must be removed from the intestines, else the latter will become discoloured. Then they are wound on frames and left to dry in the open air. The gut for a musical string must be from a lamb born in spring and slaughtered not later than October or Novem- ber of the same wear; those from older sheepcan be used only for bass strings or for minor purposes. The best quality is termed "summer gut," being from a lamb killed in summer. It is chiefly employed to make E strings, which are the most important. "Spring gut" is second quality ; though lacking tenacity, it is good enough for G, D, awl A strings. The dried intestines, when they reach the manufac- turer, are placed in earthen vats containing lye. After soaking for twenty-four hours they are unravelled. Again they are put into lye. being subjected to repeated baths n this substance. Meanwhile they are manipu- lated again and again for cleansing, the external mem- branes being removed, so as to leave only the fibrous material. They are bleached in a sulphur bath and ex- posed to the sun. The next process is to rub each string with pumice stone, to remove any inequalities and to bring it down to the requisite diameter. A polish is ap- plied by frequent wiping with olive oil. Before shipment the strings are dried, cut, rolled and assorted according to colour. They will retain their quality for an inde- finite period if inclosed in hermetically sealed boxes. The best violin strings are of Italian manufacture. The demand for them is so large that ten times as many Italian strings are sold as Italy is able to produce. Markneukirchen turns out large quantities of so-called Italian strings. Special care is taken to make them equal to the best. They are put up in Italian fashion, and sometimes are disposed of as " Padua" strings— Padua being the Italian city where musical strings are chiefly made. The intestine of the American sheep is not suitable material for musical strings, because, from the spring lamb it is too weak, and from the summer sheep it has a wart-like growth every 3 or 4 feet which unfits it for use. The climate of America isaiso too het, causing the gut to ferment before i has been throneh the requisite number of lye baths. Experts from Mark- neukirchen have settled in the United States for the purposes of practising their craft there, but for the yeasons mentioned they have not succeeded very well.

A BOOK FOR MARRIED WOMEN

The information contained in this book ought to be known by every married woman, and it will not harm the unmarried to read. The book is c ,nveniently divided into 12 chapters. The first chapter treats of the changes at puberty,ur when a girl becomes a woman. The second chapter treats of marriage from a doctor's standpoint ; points out the best age for marriage, and who should have children and who not, and furnishes useful in- formation that one can ordinarily get only from an in- tell,gent doctor. The third chapter tre its of the marriage of blood relations; and condemns such marriages as a rule. Chapter four treats of the signs of pregnancy. The filth chapter tells how a woman should live during the pregnant state. The sixth chapter treats ot mishaps and how to avoid them. The seventh chapter treats cf maternal impressions and shows that birthmarks are not clue tolongings on the part of the mother, bat rather to her poor health. The eighth chapter teaches how to have easy confinements. Certain people believe that women should bring forth in pain and trouble, but the hygienic physician says that confinements can be made comparatively easy if certain rules are obeyed ; these rules are given. The ninth chapter treats of the proper management of confinement until the baby is born. The tenth chapter tells how to treat the mother till she is up and about again. The eleventh ohapter treats of sterility Wes the main causes of it, how these may be overcome and children result. The last chapter treats of the change," a most important article tor all women over forty. The book is full of useful information and no book is wiitten which goes so thoroughly into matters relating to warned women. Some may think too much is told ; such can scarcely be the ca,e. for knowledge is power and the means oi attaining happiness. The book can t e tied from Dr. T• 11. North Trams pass Door.

Sourn Africa has again supplied South African the sensation of the week. The Troubles. sentence of death passed by the Transvaal court upon four of the prisoners who were arrested at Johannesberg after the JAMESON raid caused an immense amount of excitement in the country—for a few hours at all events. A little reflection had the effect of clearing the atmosphere to a considerable extent. It was soon seen that the verdict was nothing more than was to be expected under the circumstances. The prisoners were charged ,with the crime of treason, and it was clearly proved in the evidence against them that they had been guilty of inviting an armed invasion of the country in which they re- sided and of providing the necessary weapons for a violent revolution. The evidence in itself was no doubt sufficient to ensure a conviction by the Boer judges, but all doubt as to the verdict was at an end when the prisoners themselves pleaded guilty. The prescribed punish- ment for treason:in the Transvaal, as in most other countries, is death, and the death sentence was,therefore,a.s a matter of course passed upon the authors of the abortive Johannesberg revolution. The excitement in this country was still further allayed by the consideration that there was little probability of the verdict being carried out. What- ever else may be said of President KRUGER, it is universally admitted that he is one of the most astute and cautious of statesmen, and it was con- sidered to be in the highest degree unlikely that he wouid endanger the ' peace of South Africa, and especially the future of the Trans- vaal, by a want of clemency on the pre- sent occasion. As a matter of fact later news shows that the capital sentences were promptly commuted. It does not appear to be yet decided what form of punishment shall be in - flicted in place of that awarded by the court of law, but it is perhaps reasonable to anticipate that it will be of a moderate character. There is a great deal to be said for letting bye-gones be bye-genes in South Arica, and President KRUGER and his Government will probably carefully re- frain from displaying any,spirit of vindictiveness. At the same time a great deal will depend upon the methods adopted by the English Government in dealing with the new situation. There seems to be a danger that Mr. CHAMBERLAIN may in dealing with the Transvaal make the same mis- take that Lord SALISBURY fell into with reference to Armenia. It will be remem- bered that Lord SALISBURY soon after he came into office approached the Armenian question in fashion which seemed to suggest that force would be used if peaceable persuasion would not answer the purpose. But as a matter of fact there was no intention from the beginning to use force,and in the end Lord SALISBURY had to retire from his diplomatic encounter with the SULTAN with a diminished prestige and certainly without having seeered any alleviation of the dreadful sufferings of the Armenians. Is Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN about to repeat the same mistake in his deal- ings with PresidentiKauGeß ? The COLONIAL SECRETARY seems just now to be alternately fol- lowing two directly contrary lines of policy. At one time he fully admits the independence of the Transvaal so far as the management of its internal affairs is concerned, yet at another time he acts as though he claimed the right to dictate, or at all events, to suggest what reforms should be made by the Government at Pretoria. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN 1111 Pt really make up his mind to act consistently upon one line or the other. If he recognises the independence of the Transvaal, which is the only honest course so long as the treaty of 1884 is in force, he must rigorously refrain from publicly meddling with the internal administration of that country and confine himself to those private suggestions which President KRUGER says he is willing to receive and consider. If on the other hand he is determined to assert the " paramouutcy " of this country in internal as well as external affairs, that too is a clear and con- sistent policy although it means the violation of a solemn treaty. It means also a desperate war between this country and the Boers of the Trans- vaal and the Orange Free State and probably a civl conflict in the South African colonies between the British and the Dutch residents.

===== 0002601_18960502_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
TEE EXAMINER SATURDAY, MAY 2. 1896."

WHAT has been aptly described The Rating Bill. as the Rent-roll Relief Bill was rather roughly handled on Wed- nesday by one of the Government's own sup- porters. Mr. WHITELEY, CoLiservative member for Stockport, can see neither justice nor reason in the proposal of the Government to tax the workingmen of the towns for the benefit of wealthy rural landlords. Mr. WHITHLEy gave a plain warr.ing to his political friends of _the results of the unjust and reactionary policy they have entered upon. tlf the workmen," he said,

"believe that their interests are to be sacrificed to the interests of the country, and that they are to be regarded as pawns on the political chess-board, or as hewers of wood and drawers of water to the counties, they will give us up." But this warning will have no effect upon Lord SALISBURY and his colleagues. Although the Government secured its majority by working class votes, it may be relied upon to prove before its term of office expires that the Tory party is still, above all else, a landlord's party. Mr. WHITELEY declared that Lancashire was dead against the Government on this rating question, and lie even went so far as to offer to resign his seat so as to test the feeling of the country if any member of the Government would do the same. It is needless to say that this offer was not accepted. In order to show to what extent the towns would lose by these "grants in aid" being confined to rural districts Mr. WHITELEY made some interesting calculations to the effect that the loss to Manchester would be equal to a capital sum of £1,209,000, to Liverpool of £1,320,000, to Birmingham of £914,000, ond to Bristol of £167,000. The criti- cisms of the Bill made by Mr. FENWICK, the _miners' member from Northumberland, were also very much to the point. It has been said, for in- stance, that the Government are only acting upon the recommendation of aßoyal Commission, but Mr. FENWICK pointed out that the recom- mendations of Royal Commissions are not always so promptly acted upon. The suggestions of the Commission on royalty rents and wayleaves had, for example, been quietly ignored. Yet,lir. FEN- . WICK declared, the very people the Rating Bill was intended to relieve were those who were bleeding the mining industry to death.

The proposed meeting on the subject of bad trade in Runcorn took place this week. A very re- presentative gathering ot ratepayers and influen- tial commercial men was held, amongst whom was Sir J. T. Brunner. Many suggestions were thrown out, the chief of which was that the District Coun- cil sliould consider whether it is not possible to offer special rateable inducements to incoming manufacturers. This no doubt might be done, and combined with the natural advantages of the town should clo something to eucourage new industries in Runcorn.

The question of the Port Sanitary Authority area ' in connection with the Ship Canal is beginning to force itself to an issue, and the latest move of the Local Government Board is to threaten to take the matter to Parliament, where, according to their advice, if the Bill is passed its alteration will in the future be well nigh impoQsible. Both the Runcorn Urban and Rural District Councils have been ad vised to fight the matter at once,and that the Rural has decided to do. The Urban District Council is, however, contenting itself with sending the memo- rial as a protest, which considering that it has ad- vanced, several strong reasons why Runcorn should not be included is a somewhat weak way of shelv- ing the question. The Council, if it does want to keep out of a Sanitary Authority which is almost an insult to the town, ought to do something more than protest.
