RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RUNCORN CYCLING CLUB. A good muster of this club journeyed to Tarvin on Saturday and returned via Chester after an enjoyable outing. The half holiday section did not turn out on Wednesday,m4st of the members travelling to Broughton by train to witness the football match. LARGE VESSEL ON THU SHIP CANAL AT RUNCORN. The vessel "Westgate," of Liverpool, arrived on Monday afternoon, and is lying at the Runcorn lay-by, where a cargo of 3,000 tons of grain for Manchester is being discharged. The cargo will then be conveyed up the canal by "Salt Union" fiats. STREET ACCIDENT. On Thursday morning Mr. Charles Watson, butcher, Church-st.,was driving his pony and trap along Church- st., when the animal fell and Mr. Watson and his young son were thrown out into the road. Both escaped serious injury.

HALTON-ROAD WESLEYAN CHURCH.

The second session the thrls%Sewing Guild at this church under the presidency of Mrs. George was brought to a close on Monday. A tea party was given on that night, and the usual prizes distributed. The Rev. W. George' Wesleyan minister in charge of the church, has accepted the invitation of the steward of the Bury cir- cuit, to take charge of the Wesleyan church at Sum- merseat after the Conference of 1899. SRAIRRN'II INSTITUTE. RUNCORN. _

The Financial Committee of the above Institute met recently, and after meeting all demands in connection with the concert given by the Dusky Minstrels a satis- factory balance was left in hand. A committee has been formed to conduct future entertainments, kc., of which Mr. J. B. Ho'lands has been appointed hon. treasurer. and Mr. J. K. Digges hon. secretary. BRUNSWICK WESLEYAN SCUOOL, RUNCORN. A social gathering was held in the above school, en Thursday evening, the occasion being the closing gather- ing in connection with the Wesleyan Guild. The Rev. T. Brackenbury occupied the chair, and several brief addresses were delivered on the subject "Has the Guild doue good." The proceedings were interspersed by selec- tions by the choir. RAILWAY CONCESSION TO BOATMEN. An answer has been received by Mr. Gatley of the the Watermen's Association in reply to a memorial sent to the London and North Western Railway Company, a month ago, urging them to make some reduction in the fares between Liverpool and Runcorn,to enable boatmen whose boats were lying at Liverpool to spend the week- end at home. In reply Mr. Shaw of Lime-street, accedes to the request and a reduction for watermen from 28.61, to is. ad. will shortly be announced. INTERESTING MARRIAGE AT RUNCORN. CHA.RGE OF PERMITTING DRUNKENNESS.

The magistrates at Daresbury Petty Sessions, on Tuesday afternoon, were engaged for several hours in hearing a case, in which John Highland, an elderly farm labourer, living in a loft at Dutton, was charged with being drunk on the licensed premises of the Talbot Arms, in the occupation of John Atherton, and John Atherton (formerly superintendent ef the police at Runcornl,waa charged with having on the 4th April, at Dutton. per- mitted drunkenness on his premises known as the Tal- bot Arms.—Mr. Giles. solicitor, Chester, prosecuted on behalf of the police, and Mr. A. Browne. Warrington, defended. In opening the case, Mr. Giles said the facts were that on the 4th April,Sergeant Benfield in company with P.C. Griffiths: visited the Talbot Arms, Dirtton, and there found two men in the taproom. One of them was the defendant Highland. who was drunk, and was shouting and also knocking OA the table with his fists. Sergeant Bonfield drew the landlord's attention to the condition of Hiehfield. The landlord became excited, and told him to go on with his reporting ;he would meet him. The sergeant then said " Very well. I will report you." It appeared that Efighfield was seen by Griffiths coming out of a public house kept by a man named Whitley,thc same afternoon. Whitley refused to serve Highfiele be- cause he thought he had had sufficient, and was not a proper person to be served with drink. At the time the officers visited the Talbot Arme,Hig. field had a glass of been before him. He was noisy and drunk. In a few minutes Highfield left the House and staggered across the road. P.S. Benfield told Atherton that the man was drunk,and Mr. Atherton became a.biniive in his language rather than conciliatory. He (Mr. Giles) had nothing to say against Mr. Atberton one way or another. There was one other point. It had been said in the neighbourhood of the house that this prosecution had been taken from motives of spite.—Mr.Browne : It is the unanimous opinion of the people in the neighbourhood. —Mr. Giles continuing said Mr. Browne seemed anxious to mike a point of that. But there could really he no ground for saying that the proceedings were instituted for spite. Acting-Sergeant Benfield and P.C. Griffiths made their report to the superintendent. Superinten- dent Okell in the ordinary course of business forwarded it to the Chief Constable (Colonel Hamersley) and upon his instructions proceedings were taken. Whether Mr. Atherton could make out a case of spite he could not tell but he theueht the magistrates would come to the con- clusion that Highfield was drunk, and drunk for some time on the premises. P.S. Benfield corroborated the preceding statement, and added that as he was going out of the door Mr. Atherton followed him. He was very excited.and said, "Don't come here with your impudence." Witness told him that he was not giving him any impudence. Mr. Atherton then said, "Yu want eduction before you knew whether a man is drunk." Cross-examined by Mr. Browne: Witness stated that he had visited Mr. Atherton's place about once a week. He had never been there three times a day. There were other public-houses in his district which were visited three times a day. He had visited some houses six times a day. He was not in the habit of going to each cuetomer and staring them in the face. Mrs. Atherton did not ask him if h. had brought his box. He believed she asked P.C. Griffiths that. He knew that a bleek- smith gave Riehland permission to sleep in a loft. He did not know that Mr. Atherton practically kept fligh• land. He had been nine years in the force, and had nothing areeinst Mr. Atherton. P.C. Griffiths; also gave corroborative evidence. George Whitlow, licensee of the Red Lion Inn, Preston Brook, stated that Riehfield visited his house on Satur- day, the 4th April, between five and six o'clock. Wit- ness refused to serve him as he thought the man had had sufficient. Cross-examined, he did not say Hiehfield was drunk but he had had suffieient. Mr. Browne said that the matter might have ended before it was brought to the notice of the Chief Con- stable of Cheshire. Mr. Giles seem-d to aneloeise fir

having brought Mr. Atherton into Court, and whenever an advocate began to apllogise they might know that he did not like the job, and did not believe in his case. In Mr. Atl.l,nton they hid a man who had the, respect of almost every person in t'sat neighbourho.ad. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The annual meeting of this Board was held on Tues- day, at the Workhouse,Dutton, when there were present the Revs. Dr. Gastaldi' J. Simpson, Messrs. A. R. Nor- man,S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., T. Lea, S. Yarwood, the members of the Rural District Council, and Mr. George Ashton, deputy-clerk. APPOINTMENT OF CHAIRMAN.

Mr. Mclntyre moved that Mr. J. Knowles be re- appointed chairman.—Mr. Roylance seconded.— Dr. Gastaldi thought that every year there should be a change of chairman, and proposed Mr. Brandreth as chairman for the year.—Mr. Yarwood seconded the amendment for which nine voted. Fifteen voted for the resolution. The resolution was afterwards put and car- ried unanimously.—The Chairman thanked the Board for re-appointing him. As to the appointment of a new chairman every year he thought that it would be wiser not to bind the board to a yearly anpointment. He found that a good half of the year was gene before a new chairman could get into harness. For himself, he could say that if the chairmanship was offered to him next year, he certainly would not have it. The duties of the chair were increasing,, and he found the appoint- ment of a good vice-chairman and the advice of clerk and deputy clerk was of great assistance to the chair- man.—Mr. Woodward moved that Mr. Brandreth be re- appointed vice-chairman.—This was seconded by Dr. Gastaldi and agreed to.—Mr. Brandreth in returning his thanks for the compliment paid him expressed pleasure It gave him to see the chairman re-appointed. He thought if that appointment was made aa annual one they would not get on so well. The present chairman had attended very closely to his duties. APPOINTMENT OF COMMITTEES.

RUNCORN URBAN DISTRICT COUCIL.

THE PROPOSED PORT SANITARY ORDER.

A special meeting of the above council was called on Tuesday night to determine whether to proceed with or withdraw the memorial to the Local Government Board, with respect to the Manchester Port Order, 1896. The meeting which was held in the Town Hall was attended by the Chairman, Mr. J. W. Ligt.tburn, Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Messrs. S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., G. H. Kirkham, T. Dean, M. Sweetma.n, D. Bisbrown, J. Horsfield, W. Handley; and Mr. J. Lake (law clerk), Mr. EiMarshall (secretary) and Mr. Wilding (surveyor). The proceedings were of a private nature, the outcome of which was that the Chairman said, when the reporters returned, "we have learned that the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Council have decided to petition aga,nst the prov- isional order for the formation of a Port Sanitary Auth- ority which will necessarily mean that the Local Government Board will have to go to Parliament to get this order confirmed.lt has been thought by many of the members present to-night that under the circumstances itlwill be wise to let our memorial remain as a protest against what has been considered an injustice to Run- corn without binding ourselves in any future action.— Dr. Gastaldi : I move with pleasure that the petition against the Manchester Port Order of 1896 be not with- drawn for the reasons which are already known to us and for the benefit which may accrue in the future, because I believe the memorial sent on this subject will remain as a protest against the action taken by the Man- chester people in forming an autnority and compelling us to pay a portion of the expense of the sanitary port. —Mr. Taylor: Under the circumstances and from what we have heard, I think it is the wisest course to take and I will second the resolution.—Mr. Handley, said he would not propose an amendment. He had expressed himself pretty freely, and he would now do nothing but vote against it.—Mr.Bisbrowne similarly expressed him- self.—The Chairman wished it to be clearly understood that the Runcorn Urban District were not forcing the Local Government Board into Parliament action.—The resolution was then put and carried, Messrs. Handley and Bisbrowne voting agiinst it.

A ROAD QUESTION.

Messrs. Orme and sluniz, Limited, quarryowners, Runcorn Bill, in a letter said they had taken the south quarry and they asked to be allowed to cross the road leading to Weston Point near the houses called the Dog Karim-Is.—The matter was' referred to the Highway Committee.

THE PORT SANITARY AUTHORITY. The Local Government Board wrote with reference to the constitution of the Port eSanitary Authority of Mancliester,saying they were not prepared to reopen the ! matter with a view to the amendment of the order at the present time ; that if the objections were not with- drawn the Board would submit the order in its present form to Parliament for confirmation, and that in the event of the order becoming provisional, and being con- ferred by Parliament, future amendment would be less easy than if the order were accepted as now formed, it might be well for the District Council to consider whether it might net be desirable to withdraw their obitctions. !Mr. Hammend, writing from Ennesmare Gardens, said Sir John Brunner desires me to say that he has had an interview with Sir Hugh Owen, and advises that the objection to the order shall be withdrawn. You will remember that Sir John has understood that the charge upon Runcorn has been decided on a fair proportion by comparison of the foreign shipping frequenting the Man- chester and Runcorn ends respectively in 1894. Sir John pointed out to Sir Hugh Owen, that whereas Run- corn has been the resort of foreign shipping for 100 years Manchester has only just begun to be so used, and that it is probable that the proportion in 1894 has already been considerably altered, and will be more altered as year by year goes on. Sir Hugh has promised that as soon as you can show that the proportion ob- tained in 1894 be changed and that Manchester has in- creased, and Runcorn remained stationary/ or decreased he would be ready to reconsider the matter. If you now press your objection the tfoard will be compelled to go to Parliament for confirmation of their orders, and when the order has been completed it cannot be altered except by leave of Par- liament, whereas if • you withdraw your objection and the Urban Authority does the same the Board can at any time vary the order without going to Parliament. —The Clerk.who is in London.also wrote saying—" Mr. Heywood and I had an interview with the Local Govern- ment Board officials on the matteribut I am sorry to say we have made no impression. They in effect told us that unless we withdraw our objection to the order they should go to Parliament for the purpose of having it confirmed. We have since had an interview with Messrs. Reese and Ferreour Parliamentary agents, as to the chance of our being successful in opposing and they thought that we should stand a better chanee now of keeping out of it by opposing before a committee of the House than we should have of getting out if once we submitted and the order became law. They are, how- ever,gning carefully through the draft of the memorial and other papers to-night. One thing appears very evi- dent.if we submit now and the members of the Board representinr, Manchester and Salford should go in for an Isolation Hospital and spend a large amount in the construction as in all probability(they would,we should if in a few years time we wished to have the order recon- sidered, be met with the objection that we had been parties to the expenditure and could not be released. At any rate it seems to us, everything considered, now if ever is the time to fight against the Order. As time is pressing and we may have to act before the next meet- ing of the Council we think it would be well that a reso- lution should he passed to the effect that the clerk be in- structed to petition against the Order and do all things necessary for presentinc, sueh petition. Then if our Parliamentary agents should advise it we can, subject to the approval of the Port Sanitary Authority Committee and of the Parliamentary Committee proceed, and it on , the other hand they should not, or the two committees ! should decide that we ought not, we can desist.—Mr. Williams reviewed the subject, and mentioned that tney had protested all along against Widnes, Warrington, Ellesmere Port and other districts not b,ing included in the order, seeing that the same danger of the introduc- tion rf disease by foreign triffie applied as much to them as to Runcorn. The building of a hospital would mean the expenditure of a large sum, and if the order passed in tOe present veir, the district would be sad- dled with a considerable sum, probably £lOO a year.— Mr. Chadwick dii not think one-twentieth of the ton- nage of foreign ships came into the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Council—After a short discossion.on the motion of Mr. Williams, seconded by Mr. Chadwick, a resolution was passed in accerdanc,e with the advice of the Clerk.

not a word of any description to be said against him. He had the entire sympathy of the people in the neighbour- hood in which he lived in that case, and if he had that sympathy there was something wrong some Nhere. He was the landlord of a country public-house, and he (Mr. Browne) would ask their Worships whether they be- lieved it was necesssary for a police officer to visit it twice a day. Did it not prove that there was persecution going on? There had only been a reason for visiting it that often since Mr. Atherton became the landlord. There was an ulterior object in that case, and it was shown by the fact that since the police had found a case they had only visited the house once in four weeks. It went to prove that Benfield was a biassed witness, and that went against his credibility. Superintendent Ather- ton had had more experience in judging whether a man was drunk or not than the sergeanz., and that was what Mr. Atherton meant when he told Ben- field that he wanted educating. The normal condition of Highfield was such that it was difficult to tell whether he was drunk or not. Even at his work when he had not tasted beer, he talked about Napoleon. He was a peculiar subject to judge fairly or properly whether he was sober or drunk. He had worked for Mr. Mounfield, and on the Saturday in question he was Riven a drink of what was known as "farmer's beer," which was very weak. He had only earned is. 9d. during the week* At Mr. Atherton's house he was served with one glass of beer by Miss Atherton. He wanted more but Mrs. Atherton told him that he was too noisy. The empty glass was taken away from him. It was a deli- berate untruth on the officer's part to say that there was a glass containing beer before him when they entered the house. _ _

Mrs. Atherton, wife at the defendant, said the police had hardly been away from the house since they went to it. Highfield was net drunk, he appeared to be just as he was that morning. He had never any means to buy beer. Evidence in support of gr. Browne's statement was also given by Miss Elizabeth Athertov, Miss Amy Atherton, Mr. J. Morris Rowe, farm labourer, Mr. Wal- ter Curzon, platelayer, Thomas Lightfoot, and Mr. James Farrington. The latter stated that Highfield was inebriated but not drunk. In answer to ques- tions by Mr. Giles, witness admitted tbat he spoke tonthe police officers, and told them that he could not refute their statement that the man was drunk, "knowing the subject." He told the officers to treat Mr. Atherton as they would another publican. It was a great mistake when the "super" took the house that there were not two fresh police officers.—Mr. George Mounfield, farmer, Preston Brookond Mr. David Walker also gave evidence.—Mr. John Atherton said he complained of the persecution of the police. In his opin- ion there was no necessity for the repeated visits of the police. He did say to the officers " Why do you haunt me in this manner, you had better come and sit On the hob." Cross-examination, he said he had been at the house about three mostbs. He instructed his officer 4to visit public houses occasionally—now and again ; that was whenever they thought fit. He could not say how often that would be. He recommended Bonfield for the position of acting-sergeant. He was a man of judgment aud conducted himself properly. He admitted that he told Bentiald to get educated to become acquainted with cases and not to call sober men drunk. The magistrates dismissed tbe case against both parties.

WESTON POINT.

ACCIDENT.—Late on Monday afternoon H. Millingtonof , Eaton-st.. Runcorn, was geriously injured while fol- lowing his emollyincot. He was working on a steamer at Weston Point at the dock of Mr. Branch, engineers when the chain slinc!ing the stern end slipped and he was struck. Millington was knocked into the water, but quickly pulled out by Mr. Gorge Cooke. He was then found to be severely injured on one leg, and was seen by Drs. Ormsuy and McLennan, who advised his removal, o the hospital at Li verpool.

RUNCORN CO-OPORATIVE SOCIETY.

ANNUAL TEA. PARTY AND ENTERTAINMENT.

This annual event took place in the Public Hall, on Wednesday evening, about 250 persons sitting down to tea. The tables were presided over by Mesdames Hartles, Bowyer, Rogers. Cosier, Dudley, Leadstone, William Brown, Mdtoree, Dyer, Rushton, Stubbs, Dean, Balshaw, Upton. T. Bowyer, Morris and Smith, and Misses Cosier, Rhodes, Hough, Dudley, Leadstone, Moores, Riley, Hesford and Stubbs. Sir John Brunner presided over the after proceedings, and WaS supported on the platform by the following Members of the committee. Messrs. Dudley, Rhodes. Riley, Leadstone, Stubbs, Dyer, Bowyer, Moores, Dean, Hartle's. Cosier and William W. Upton (secretary), and W. M. Morris (manager). There were also present Mr. Lander, of the Co-aperative Wholesale Sot:ley and Mr. Birchen. J.P., of Warrineton. The Chairman (Sir J. T. Brunner) field he wanted to say before he went any farther with the proceedings that night that the whole of the population of Runcorn with the excsntion of those before him were waiting near the Post Office in order to hear the result of the great foot- ball match (laughter and i.pplaese) which they confi- dently expected to be pleasant for Runcorn people—and if he was irrmitted to say so—very pleasant news for himself too. He found on opening the programme that Part I. began with the chairman's address. He was very much puzzled to know what to say to them for the reason that he had been thinking of late of a great many matters. hut not specially of co-operation. He had been thinking about education, local rating upon agricultural land and urban land—two very important matters occupying the present attention of the House of Com- mons. Upon either of these subjects without notes he could deliver them a very long address, but luckily for them the occasion did not call for him to do so. He was pleased to be there in order to show to them his hearty sympathy with the work done by the Society (applause) and he was still more pleased to have met that evening two of the original founders of the Co-operative Society who met in the year 1862 for the purpose of starting it. He referred to Mr. Rhodes and Mr. Riley and be was pleased to see them there looking little older than when they first started the work, and he found that Mr. Riley was still an enthusi- astic that he attended 32 meetings out of 35 possible attendances during last year. They would forgive him (Sir John) If he mingled a little admonition with his address for he would not be a true friend if he did not say what he felt. There WAR a matter in which they foil short of their high ideal. This society according to the heading of the half yearly report was established for the domestic and intellectual advancement of the mem- bers. Looking at the record of the moneys received and the moneys p +id out, and the dividends earned on the large amount of share capital, though from a domestic point of view it had been successful, vet he did not find a record of much done for the intellecteal advancement of the members. (Hear. hear.) He knew that the town was suffering depression in trade and he was not going to press them to spend any of their hard earned money at the present time in this direc- tion but, he would appeal to them to support their com- mittee when, as he felt would happen before long, they made a proposal to them that they should follow the ex- ample of many other admirable societies throughout the country, and try to do something collectively for the intellectual advancement of members. He appealed to them to support their committee heartily in that pro- posal. (Applause.) He was very pleased to see towards the end of the report that the hours of closing were early. The House of Commons was now engaged in the consideration of a Bill, to provide for the compulsory closing of shops at earlier hours. If the members of the great and important house in Lendon only knew what work the people did for themselves when they managed their own businesses and how those businesses were managed, he thought that they would be encouraged to try to persuade the many good people engaged in the retail trade to follow their example, and to allow their employees soma time for innocent re- creation and some time for intellectual advancement He considered, though they did not come up to the highest ideals, there war good work being done by the society in Runcorn. It raised many a family frsm in- dieence,and had done a greet deal to help Runcorn over similar depressions in trade before. His mine was car- ried back to the fact that it was only on Tuesday that he met and discussed with their city fathers one scheme after another to draw trade to the town, and it had oc- curred to him that if the members of the local authority made it known to all who might possibly introduce manufactories in the town, that in the members of the Co-operative Society they had a body of extremely thrifty workmen, intending manufacturers would be very mirth more inclined to try theirrfortunes amongst them in Runcorn. (Applause.) Mr. Lander also addressed the meeting and said the pleasure of his visit was enhanced very much by the fact that it was a very rare thing to find a member of the House of Commons who had time to deal with Co-opera- tive questions. It was a little surprising at this stage of their national existence when social reform was so keen and the candidates to carry out those reforms were many, that so few members of Parliament accociated themselves with the practical working of a movement• which he ventured to say had done and was doing more to improve the social and industrial condition of the masses of the people than any other movement that had been known. (Hear, hear.) Speaking of the educational side of the question he thought the Runcorn Society had fallen short of their ideals. The society with Which he was connected had always given 2 per cent, of their profit to the cause of federation and the result was that they had a lihrary which was far superior to anything which the munici- pality pwessed. (Applause.) The interesting con- cert programme was hen opened by Mrs. Beesl.y who gave a fine rendering of the song " Bid me discourse." Mr. Beesely, was followed by Mrs. Timperlr who received an encore for her song "Tit for Tat," Miss Fleatherins,,ton's song. "Say Au revoir," was well veeived as was Mr. Dockery, whose effort" Come in the Garden Maud "was given in excel- lent style. Miss Cosier gave a sweet rendering of "Two spoons," Mr. C. 'Atherton was applauded for his song " Moreatty." and Miss Timherley's song " Loves Golden Dream," was also warmly received. Mr. Grounds was encored for his song." My first wife." Mr. Dudley, proposed a vote of thanks to the Chair- man and Mr. Rhodes seconded. The Chairman in responding, said that he had listened to the speeches with great interest, and had learned' good deal. Mr Cosier' proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Rander, and Mr. Riley seconded. A vote of thanks was also accorded the artistes and helpers on the motion of Mr. Birchen. Mr. A. C. le Coutre accompanied the singers.

MAKE LIFE ENJOYABLE.

Loss of health means loss of all which makes life en- joyable ; for, in addition to the bodily gain and suffering which it cause, it entails a vast amount of mental pain and anxiety, especially in the homes of the working classes. It brings in its train a host of discomforts which retard the recovery of the sufferer, and hinder, instead of assisting, Nature to perform her functions. When a working man suffers from any ailment which prevents his following his daily avocation, he is natur- ally filled with anxiety regarding the maiatenanco of those depending on him for their daily sustenance. His very love for his family retards and hinders his speedy recovery, and his spirits become depres: ed at the antici- pation of the gloomy prospects before them in thd event of a long continuance of his illness. It is well known that depression of spirits often proves a serious bar to a speedy recovery.

These results arise in addition to the various incon- veniences and discomforts which the sufferer and his family experience as direct results of his illness, such as additional expenses for luxuries for the sick, with in- creased difficulty to obtain those luxuries, or even the necessities of life.

Most of the various forms of disease which trouble the labouring classes of the country, and which are often attributable, more or less, to some unhealthly condition of their daily work, may be classed under a few leading beads.

To avoid thl discomforts arising from various forms Of disease,every working man should seek the aid of THE VEGETABLE TONIC,

Which has gained a world-wide renown as the most wonderful medicinal discovery of the age; and as un- failing remedy it is prescribed by mauy leading physi- cians in various ailments.

For Bronchitis, Asthma, Coughs, and Colds, Shortness of Breath, Neuralgia, General Weakness, Low Spirits, Nervousness, Indigestion, Influenza.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

AN INDECENT ADVERTISEMENT. —lla,tvr PENALTY. At the Newton Petty &Lesions on Saturday, before Mr. T. J. Gillespie and Colonel Wond,John William Muskett, of Warrington, and two youths named James William Owen and John Isaacs,wEre charged with having on the 13th inst.at Newton,delivered to the inhabitants certain printed matter which was of an indecent and obscene nature, under the Indecent Advertisement Act.—Mr. Stuart proaecuted,and Mr. HI. Longton defended.—Mr. Stuart stated that on the date named Sergeant Bancks and P.C. Burns were on duty in Market-st., Earlestown, about 5 p.m. when they saw the three defendants dis- tributing handbills. On seeing the kind of literature te- ing distributed they apprehended the defendants under the Indecent Advertisement Act. Several tradesmen told the officers that they were extremely glad that the police were taking proceedings owing to the indecent nature of the matter distributed. In consequence of the address on the bill,Sergeant Bancks went and saw a manUamed Hammond at the address given in Warrington. Ham- mond told the police-sergeant that he thought be was doing no wrong in sending the bills out,but if he was,he would not do so again, and would destroy those he had in stock. He admitted that he had instructed the three defendants to deliver the bills. Mr. Stuart suggested that the magistrates should inflict the heaviest penalty against Hammond. A great deal of immorality might be caused by people following the advice given.and tho-re was no telling where the matter mieht end.—Sergeant Bancks corroborated Mr. Stuart's statement.—ln cross- examination witness was pointing out the in- decent pissages, when the Chairman said he objected to the whole circular. -- P.C. Burns deposed to seem; the band bills distributed in shops in Market-st. In cross-examination he paid the handbills were floating about the street wholesale. He picked one up.—Mr. Langton for the defence submitted that the handbill contained nothing indecent or obscene within the meaning of the Indecent Advertisement Act —The Clerk said he should advise the magistrates that there was.—The Chairman : The whole thing is filthy from beginning to end.—Mr. Stuart said one man was corrupting a whole neighbourhood.—Continning, Mr. Longton said Mr. Hammond would prove that the bills had been delivered in a number of Lancashire townsond this was the first time the police bad taken objection to them. He asked the magistrates to deal leniently with the defendants as it was the first offence.—A fine of 54. and costs was imposed on each defendant.—Thnma.s Hammond, described on- tbe handbill as " Profa,sor Hammond, Botanic House, Warrington," and carrying on business at 33, litattermarket-st., WAS charged with causing the bills to be distributed.—Mr. Longton said as the police had objected to the bills, the defendant had destroyed them, and ne more would be issued. He asked that the defendant should be reasonably dealt with.—A fine of £2 and coats, £5 16s. in all was imposed. TEN BAND-PLATING IN THE PARK.-••WARNING TO DISORDERLY PERSONS.

At tbe Warrington Police Court on Monday morning a young man named George Smith, 19 Cartwright-st.. was charged with being guilty of disorderly behaviour in the Park on the 15th April, contrary to the bye-laws of the borough.—P.C. Corbett said that on the day in question the Volunteer band was performing in the Park and there was a large number of people there. Witness heard a noise as of crackers, going off and asyhe had re- ceived a complaint from two ladies who had been frightened by crackers he went in search of the cause of the noise. After some time he came across defendant and watching him closely he caught him in the act of letting a cracker off.—Defendant said be did not know be was doing wrong.—He was fined ss. and costs.—The Mayor condemned defendant's conduct and said he wished the public to know that if that kind of thing went on in the Park the full penalty of the law would be enforced upon those caught at it.—Mr. Monks: Tie full penalty is 403. and costs or a month's hard labour. and the public have the right to be protected at these performances. They (the magistrates) would feel it their duty to protect the public as well as they could. No other defendant would get off with ss. and costs. OBSTRUCTION 'AT BANK QUAY STATION.

Before the Mayor, Alderman Harrison, Mr. F. Monks, 1 and Mr. J. W. Hayward, at the Warengton Borough Court on Monday, Jamea Leslie, Arthur, John. and Charles Heaton, Thomas Durkin, and John Whittaker, were charged on remand with loitering about Bank Quay Station.—The Chief Constable said the case was ad- journed a month ago that day on the defendants giving an understanding not to obstruct in the neighbourhood of Bank Quay Station. There were two men present re- presentim the Railway Co., who would state how de- fendants had kept—or failed to keep—their promises.— Charles Parsons, detective in the employ of the London and North-Western Railway Co., said that during the last ten days he had cautioned John Heaton, Charles Heaton and Durkin, to keep away from the Station which they had refused to do. He had seen them ob- struct passengers.—Ticket-collector Holland said he had seen all the defendants since the adjournment, about the station, and some of them came into the large room of the station and interfered with the passengers.—Mr. Monks asked if the six licensed porters were enough for the wants of the station, and Holland replied in the affirmative. He (Mr. Monks) said also that he thought it ought te be made as easy as possible ' for a respectable man to get a badge as outside porter.—Mr. Talbot said all the defendants had bad characters, and, therefore, could not get alicense. Leslie at one time was an out- side porter until be was convicted of felony when he had the badge taken from him. He had no wish to appear vindictive but he must prosecute if the men would not do right. —The Mayor: We gave them a chance and they now compel us to do something.—Defendants were fined 405. and costs or in default one month's hard labour. ASSAIILTTNG A SISTER.—EXEMPLARY PENALTY. Before Messrs. Garnett, Guest and Smith at the War- rington Berough Court, on Tuesday. William Curley, a disreputablP-looking fellow, was charged in custody, baying been apprehended on a warrant, with commit- ting an ageravated assault upon his sister Catl- erine Curley. on the previous afternoon at about one o'clock. —The Chief Constable said this was a case of" another brutal assault by a strong man upon a weak woman." He went on to say that about neon on the previous day—between twelve and one o'clock—a young girl, the sister of the prisoner, went home from her work to get some dinner. When she arrived she found her brother lying liven the sofa in his accustomed state of drunken- ness. The prisoner was to all appearances a loafer, and was always more or less under the influence of drink. Angry words ensued between the prisoner and his mother soon after the daughter's entrance, and he attempted to strike the old lady across the head with a -cup. The daughter.. rushed between them and inter- cepted the blow, trying afterwards to part them. She was net successful, and her brother next picked up a

heavy tin can used for tea. With this he attempted to strike his mother, but again the daughter intervened and shielded ber. Then the prisoner turned his anger upon his sister and struck her a least one violent blow upon the head with the can, cutting open the skull. Blood flowed freely from the wound.and she was taken to the Infirmary So serious did he (the Chief Constable) consider the as- sault that he authorised the arrest of Curley without a warrant, fearing that the young girl might succumb to her injuries. Fortunately, however, for the prisoner, she bad suriived, and moreover was able to visit the court that morning and give evidence.—Catherine Curley, whose head was much bandaged, and who ap- peared to be in a very weak state, entered the witness- hex and corroborated the statement of the Chief Con- stable. All that he had said she asserted was correct; and she supplemented hi; remarks by saying that her brother bad never warked for the past two months. Only a week ago he had come out of gaol, and had been drunk ever since. He had frequently threatened to take witness's life, and also the life of her mother—Prisoner denied being a "loafer," adding that he had worked whenever he could get a chance, and could produce from Mr. Isherwood a five years' character.—The Chief Con- stable : He speaks of " a five years' character." I can give him one ranging over a much longer period than that. Mr. Talbotthen proceeded to read the convictions that had been recordsd against prisoner, after which the Bench committed him to gaol for six months with hard labour. A DIVIDED HOUSEHOLD.

At the Warrington Borough Court on Tuesday Jane Johnson appeared in support of a summons against her stepsonjohn James Johnson, for assaulting her on the 25th inst. Jane Johnson stated, that the prisonpr as- saulted her seriously.and hit her in two or three parts of the body.—The husband of the complainant expressed a wish to give evidence as to the cause of the quarrel, whereupon• Jane turned round and said " Go back,tha,'s been drinking since six o'clock this morning."—The husband (shouting) ; She hit him over the head, your worships, with a basin, and cut his head open.—The Bench permitted the man to come forward and give evi- dence. It was clearly seen, however, that he was very excited. He stated that his son had only come- to bor- row a shilling from him and" Jane said he had no busi- ness in the house."—Jane Johnson : I never said any such thing.—Witness continuing, eaid his wife was the worst character in Warrington. and had cut the lad's head open with a basin. He had had to get her down on the floor,or she would have murdered him. She was not fit to le at large on this earth." (Laughter.)—Mr. Garnett : How is it then that you took her for a wife? —Witness: Oh! I didn't know what she was at tho time. (Laughter.)—The Bench bound the prisoner over for six months in two sureties of £5 each, or in default a month's imprisonment, and ordered him to pay the costs.'

WARRINGTON " A " v. RUNCORN " A."—The last match of the season and the fourth match this season between the above teams was played at Runcorn cn Saturday in fine weathersbefore a moderate gathering of spectators. Warrington opened the game,Runcorn quickly returning irata touch. From the throw out the homesters got well in the visitors' ground, and Gregory, receiving a pass, scored. He tried to convert but tailed. Foe home men sent the ball to the Warrington end, but Scott smartly returned to Ur- other end. The ball wis again brought close to the visitors' goal,and from a scrimmage near the line, Gregory got possession and attempted to a dropped goal, but the ball just went outside the posts. Scott got away, but being pressed, he passed, and Wil- liamson (Runcorn) received the ball, which he sent in touch. Houghton picked up and got well off before he was tackled. The forwards tried to take the advantage, but Price promptly relieved. Another smart run f rem centre by Houghton was checked by his being thrown in touch at the Warrington quarter flag. From a scrim- mage that took place there, Percival dashed away and almost scored,being held up on the line. Play returned to centre, where Warrington were awarded a free kick, which Randles toA, and Pendlebury made his mark from it, Lightfoot kicked just as the half time whistle blew, but the ball went a long distance out. On resuming, Warringtan got into the home quar- ters for a short time, but Runcorn aided by one of Houghton's long kicks, got to the Warrington line,where several scrimmages ensued. Runcorn were frequently very near scoring but the visitors were on the alert and defended well, Scott eventually affording great relief by taking the ball beyond centre. For a few minutes pre- y ous to time the game consisted of long kicks by both sides. The match ended with Runcorn winning by one try to Warrington nil. Warrington played better dur- ing the second half than the first, and some good run- ning and passing was witnessed. Houtzhton, the inter- national full back, did a great deal for Runcorn with his long kicks. The following players were on the field : Warrincgtom : Spruce, full back; Moores, Scott, Foden and Radford, threequarters ; Price and Clarke, half backs ; Sudlow, Hughes, Royle,Randles, Freeth,Scholtz-, Bailey and Swift, forwards. Runcorn : Houghton, full back; Gregory, Lightfoot, W. Gayter, and • Williamson, three quarters; Percival and Took, half backs ; Shaw, Waslaburne, Wood, Richardson, Pendlebury, Taylor, Dyer and F. Jones, forwards.

THE SHIP CANAL BILL.

DECISION OF THE COMMITTEE.

The House of Commons Committee appointed to con- sider the Manchester Ship Canal Bill on Monday, after an inquiry extending over nine days,gave its decision on the portion of the bill relating to the tidal openings from the canal into the Mersey estuary. The members of the committee were Mr. Blake (chairman), Mr. Samuel (Stockton-on-Tees), Mr. Heywoud Johnstone, and Mr. Fardell.

The Chairmtn said : The committee have come to a resolution that so much of the preamble as relates to the tidal openings is proved. The proposal of the promoters is to do away with the tidal openings, and, in substitu- tion for the present operations, to torn out as much water as possible, as high up as possible, consistently with the reduction to 14ft. 2M. of the canal level, within 41 hours, as near as may be done, at latest within five hours, after each high tide at the Weaver sluices. The Proposed plan is (a) to open Walton lock on the fiood, as soon as the comparative levels allow of the discharge from the canal into the river, and to turn out there. for about ths space of an hour, as much water ,s possible, having regard to the changes of level oicasioned by the proposed operations below ; (b) to open Old Bandies very soon after Walton, mast to turn out there, till the canal level is brought down to 14ft. 21n., as much water as possible, having regard on the one hand to the objsct of getting out water at Walton,and on the other hand to the necessity of using the lower sluices to reduce the canal level in time ; ( ) to open the sluices at the side locks at Runcorn, Bridgewater, an.] Weston Mersey as early, and turn out there as much water as possible, having regard to the operations at Walton and Old Randles ; (d) to open the Weaver sluices for such time, and to such extent, as shall, with the aid of the other discharges, effect the prescribed reduction of the canal level within the precribed time. The alternative sug- gestion from Liverpool, as from their point of view a lesser evil, is that the retardation needed to use Walton should not take place, and that the discharge should begin at Old Randles as soon as the level there allows, thus saving an hour, and that the prescribed reduction should be accomplished an hour earlier. Of course the promoters will bring up clauses to carry out the various concessions appearing in the minutes, which we need not recapitulate. Let them bring up an amendment to clause 4, providing that the closing works are to be such as will be easily and quickly removable. Let them bring up clause- (a) creating an obligation, under a penalty of £5OO a day, to reduce the level within the time prescribed ; (b) to dc so by opera- tions such as are above described; and (c) modifying the Weaver discharge clause. If they desire, they may bring up a clause giving power to the Conservators to autho- rise, after trial, and in case of failure of the described plan, the adoption of the alternative plan, the alterna- tive plan being, of course, that of the Randles sluices. With regard to the position of the compensation claims, and with reference to the efficacy of any finding under them, that will be deferred, pending the reply of the pro- moters. The Widnes and Hutchinson Estate clauses are to apply to the status quo under the former Act. The Committee do not see their way to a recognition of the claims of the Shropshire Union, or others in like case. At the request of the promoters, consideration of certain proposals of the Runcorn Rural District authority is de- ferred. If the promoters desire they may bring up for consideration a clause for the deposit with some cne per- son for the behoof of all petitioners entitled for the re- cord. of the working of the sluices. Mr. Blennerhasset, counsel for the Widnes and Hutch- inson oppositions, intimated that he would not take part in the discussion of clauses, thereby reserving the right to oppose in the House of Lords.

The Select Committee of the House of Commons which has heard the evidence for and against the Manchester Ship Canal Company's (Tidal Openings) Bill met again on Wednesday. Counsel for the landowners who claim ' protection and compensation in reference to the drainage ot their lands raised objections with regard to the ability of the Company to pay compensation,one learned gentle- man declaring that the Company was utterly and hope- lessly bankrupt. Reference was made to the position of the first debenture holders, whose security has priority over the Manchester Corporation debentures; and the Committee declined to insert clauses which would have given the landowners who are opposing the bill rights as preference creditors. An important discussion followed with regard to the powers of the Mersey Commissioners, and the Committee finally made an important modifiea- tien of their former decision by making the assent of the Commissioners a condition precedent to the closing of the tidal openings.

A TRAC'FION ENGINE. —John Priestner sum- moned George Davies for wilfully damaging his potatoes to the extent of 45., on the 2 !Ist a, d 22nd Amil and also for assaulting him on the first mentioned date. George Davies issued a cross-summons against John Priestner for assaulting him on April 21st. Mr. Giles, of Chester, appeared • for P,lestner. John Priestner said he was a farmer at Frodsham and had been 13 years in his present term. The road leading to the farm was a private one and part of the expense of maintainance was borne by him. On Tuesday. April 21st, his traction engine way lying on one side of the road, though there was seven feet space to pass it in. Defendant (Davies) drove down the road in a milk float and went straight across his potatoes. He caged to Davies and Davies replied "Shift your engine onto: the road." On returning the same way, Davies again crossed the potatoes and he caught hold of the horse's head. Davies then jumped out of the float. and struck him and they afterwards struggled together and fed to the ground. They were separated by a man named Helsby. The day following Davies again went across his potatoe field.— Ralph Harrison and Samuel Sherlock. employees of Priestner, corroborated and stated that there was sufficient room for Davies's float to pass the engine.—William Helsby also gave corroborative evidence. For the defence Davies alleged that he could not get past the engine and was forced on to the field. On returning he got out to lead his horse back and Priestner 'truck him first. He admitted the straggle after- ward..—Davies was ord.red by the magistrates to pay the 4s. damage together with 1,..s and costs which included the advocate's fee. FOOTBALL.

WARRINGTON v. HALIFAX.

The last match in the regular list ef fixtures was played on Saturday, when Halifax who was keeping close upon the heels of Manningham, paid a visit to the Wilderspool enclosure. Warrington had a very strong team selected, but unfortunately, at the last moment four of them cried off, and the reserves also not being available, but a poor team went into the field. There were fully 7,000 spectator 3 present when the match be- gan, and the weather was all that could be desired from a spectator's point of view, though it was uncomfort- ably hot for the players. Mr. Bronkshaw of S'ocknort was the referee and the following were the players under h;a control :—Warrington : Evans, full back ; Barber Isherwond,O'Brien and Lawless,three qnarters ; Fliggin- hettom and Houghton, half hacks ; Andrews, Taylor, D tkin, Fairhurst. Berry, Wilcock, Savage, and Burrows, forwards. Halifax : Toothill, full back ; Jackson. Bottenaley, Garside, and Firth, three quarters : Arnold and Rive', half backs; Riley, A. Wilson, H. Wilson, Robertshaw, Mellor, Whitley, Dickenson, and Kitson, forwards. Taylor kicked off and during the first half Warrington had to face the sun, but there was very little wind to interfere with the game. Arnold made his mark in response to the kick ' off, and kicked in touch near the centre flag. The ensu- ing scrimmage was in favour of Warrington and Hig- gMbnttom taking advantage of this gave his side still further advantage by a judicious kick ia touch. At length a good run and kick by Smith caused the game to be fought in neutral ground where Lawless made his mark, but this d;d not prove of much advantage to his side. Some stiff scrimmages ensued here-abouts, and Isherwood by missing a pass from Fligginbottom almost let Halifax through, but O'Brien just stepped in,in time, and kicked in touch at the centre. The Halifax for- wards, headed by Riley. and the two Wilsons headed a ' dribble, and things looked had for Warrington, when Isherwood pluckily robbed them of the ball and kicked in touch. This was followed by a scrimmage for which Ring made a grand run along touch, O'Brien at length proving his downfall by knocking him in touch. Immediately afterwards, Firth got pessession but on being challenged by Lawless, he kicked too hard, and Evans touched down. After the kick-out, offside tactics caused Warrinoten to he penalised, and Rigg just missed landing a penalty coal. Firth again proved too eager after this let off for Warringtem,and again kicked the ball too far, another miner resulting. The scoring was at length opened, when Warrington ere again p.n. alisecl in a similar position to the last penalty, -and Firth landed a goal, thus putting Halifax three points ahead. Warrington by some Rend rushes by Barber, Taylor, and Dakin, roused some hope of better things after this, for an attack was made on the Halifax lines. This proved only brief ,however,snme luelcy flying kicks which Evans failed to return, giving the Tykes an advantage. 'foot- hill now got the ball clear and raced in front of the goal to drop,but he failed to ehoot straight and the ball went far wide, and Lawless kicked safely into touch. Toothill was almost over after a wild kick by Lawless, Barber just collaring him in time. From the ensuing scrimmage Rice passed out to Garside and the latter was very lucky in just getting over at the corner. Robertshaw landed a splendid goal from touch. From Taylor's kiek off, Barber and Houghton were very prominent in a passing run, and from now to half time, play was very even, Halifax then leading by eight points to nil. Rigg re- etarted and owing to a poor response by Warring. ton, Halifax were attacking immediately. Evans got them out of the difficulty by a long kick which was followed up well by Barber who was aside, and he tackled Toothill before he could reply, and play was located at the centre. Barber was again very prominent by a tricky dribble along the touch and Halifax lines were in great danger until Jackson got the ball into safe quarters, and Bottomley and Kitson dribbled down to the other end. Iligginhottom was the eaviour of his side this time,ancl for a long period the play which suc- ceeded was dreadfully monotonous, scrimmaging being the order of the day,Warrington having by far the best of it. The monotony was relieved by one fine outburst of passing between Fligginhottom, O'Brien, Barber and Lawless,but Firth put an end to this,and the play again resumed its old monotony. Warrington had dreadfully hard lines on one occasion when Berry and Dakin took the ball to the line,and Dakin actually crossed it,but was knocked back again. This was Warrington's last chance, and during the concluding sta,es of the game, they had great difficulty in keeping their lines intact, as Halifax appeared to have got their second wind. Nothing further of interest took place,however, and a very unin- teresting gam, enden in a victory for Halifax by a goat and a penalty goal (eight points) to nil,

WARRINGTON V. PRESTON —The opening match of both these clubs took place at West Cliff Grounds, Preston,on Saturday, before a good attendance. Winning the toss the Warringtonians took first batting on the capital wicket, Messrs. J. J. Crosfield and E. Rowley being opposed by Sutton and W. F. Best. Runs came slowly at the onset, and after Crosfield had cut Best for tour, a clinking ball from the Preston professional upset the deputy-captain's leg stump. Thewlis filled the vacancy, but the bulk of the scoring was done by the amateur, several late cuts earning for him repeated applause. Thewlis batted stylishly, but was eventually tempted to launch out at Best, with fatal results,for Coward judged the catch in clever style, and the Warrington professional was taken near the screen. W. H. Porlmora came next, but never seemed comfortable with the bowling, and at 43 Sutton sent him back. With J. Callon joining Row- ley a capital stand was male, and the t .tal had reached 58 when Eastwood relieved Best. The new bowler at once met with success, for Rowley was taken by the wieket•keeper btanding back. Mr. Rowley's 39 was a masterly exposition, and his contribution had not a faulty stroke. Bibby fell in Eastwood's next over, so that half the wickets were down for 53 runs. A stand was next made by S. M. Crosfield and J. Callen, and several risky runs were effected with success. However the visiting captain paid the penalty at last,for his com- panion failed to answer a call, and before Mr. Crosfield could regain his crease the bails had been removed. E. Connor joined Callon, and these two defied the efforts of the local for a lengthened period, Connor especially batting brilliantly. Changes were tried in the attack with futile effect, until at 98 Callon was taken by Todd at the wicket, his contribution of 22 being a useful addition. G. Hampson joined Connor, and an unexpected stand was made, and it was not until 138 had been reached that Hampson drove one into the hands of mid-off, the ball being safely held. This partnership realized 40 runs. The last wicket was pro- ductive of ten runs, when L. A. Voisey was howled, E. Connor carrying out his bat for a splendid 38, and in- cluded in which were five fours. Warrington's essay realized 148. Preston were first represented by Messrs. W.F. Best and G. Harper, the bowling being started by Messrs. E. Connor and J. J. Crosfield. The first-named 'batsman never seemed easy with the deliveries of the medium pace bowler, and at seven Crosfield got through the ex-Kent player's defence. A. M. Hollins joined Harper, and runs were difficult to obtain, so close was the fielding. At 23 Scrimshaw relieved Con- nor, and in his second over had the satisfaction of send- ing back Hollins with a grand length ball, whilst in the next over a brilliant catch at slip got rid of Coward,the third wicket faging at 36. Harper had a useful partner in F. H. Hollins.but little could be done with Scrimshaw and at 47 the initial batsman was taken at cover slip in fine style by Rowley, having made 23, the highest score for his side. A good stand was made by J. Leigh and G. Harper, and a couple of hits for five raised the en- thusiasm of the locals to a high pitch. However,a smart piece of fielding sent 'Leigh back, whilst the first ball from S. M. Cresfield dismissed Sykes. Clemesha showed signs of staying until at 94 Connor was given another trial, and he soon finished off the annings,a brilliant Tun- ing catch by Thewlis sending back Eastwood, whilst Sutton failed to time ar extra fast one from Connor. Todd was snapped by Hampson in clear style, and the innin.,s closed 48 runs is arrear, Warrington thus win- ning handsomely. The visitors showed superior cricket all round, and the wicket-keeping of Hampson made a favourable impression. Score and bowling analysis : WaRRINGTOX. WARRINGTON TRAINING COLLEGE.

The students at tile Warrington Training College gave their annual concert before Sir John Bridge (examiner) and a select audience in the lecture room at the College on Thursday last week. Mendelssohn's oratorio" Elijah" was chosen for representation. In venturing upon the performance of this work, the students had an arduous, and perhaps too ambitious task. The difficult and ex- acting music of this,the choicest of Mendelssohn's works, taxed the resources of the students—numbering about one hundred—to the uttermost. The principal vocal- ists amongst the students were Miss E. Eaton, Miss K. Rose. and the Misses Bennett, Creswell, Hey- wood. Hickman,. and Trotter. It is not our intention to attempt a critique of the performance ; that must be left in the hands of Sir John Bridge in his exami- nation renort. Mention may, however, be made of the valuable assistance rendered, especially in the choruses, by male members of the Warrington Musical Society, and the Parish Church choir, without which the performance would, of course.have been very incomplete. The majestic chorus "Thanks be to God," and also " Behold ! God the Lord passed by !" call for special mention, both being given with power and finish that was highly appreciated. The principal share of the solo work of "Elijah" devolves upon the titular part,and puts a great tax even on the physical powers. Mr. J. Hargreaves Hudson,ot the Cathedral,Liverpool,had been engaged for the title role, and he was fairly auccew- ful, his best efforts being in the recitative and air, "Draw near, all ye people: e^me to me !" and the air, " It is enough, 0 Lord." Mr. J. Parkinsm, of Warring- ton, sang several tenor solos, and he also took part in a double quartette in company with Mr. J. Abdey, Mr. R. Eccles, Mr. P.C. Price, and four of the students. The orchestra consisted of Mr. R. H. Pearson (Ist violin),Mr. C. H. Clare (2nd violin), Mr. G. Johnson (viola), Mr. Edgar Balmford (violoncello), Mr. R. Whitfield (double bass), Rev. Morley Stevenson, principal (piatioforte),and Mr. W. A. Wood .(harmonium). Although rather a small orchestra, the instrumentalists played the accompani- ments in a manner that heightened the effectiveness of both solo and choral work, and added much to the enjoy- ment of the audience. Due credit must be accorded Mr. W. S. Woods, the teacher of music at the College, who skilfully wielded the baton thriughout the perform- ance.

DOMESTIC TRAGED-Ii NEAR BRISTOL,

- A shocking case of attempted murder, followed by suicide, occurrei at Winterbourn9, near Bristol, on Wed- nesday. After a quarrel about some family property between Robert Smith, aged 66, and his sister, Elizabeth Smith, Robert visited the latter's house, and nothing further was heard until sometime afterwards, when Miss Smith staggered into the road, and stated that her brother attacked her with a hammer, leaving her in- sensible. On search being made in the outbuildings the lifeless body of the man was found hanging to a beam. Miss Smith is under treatment at the hospital. FOOTBALL.

THE NORTHERN UNION.

LANCASHIRE SECTION.-DECIDING CONTEST. RUNCORN v. OLDHAM.

These clubs met on Wednesday evening at the Brough- ton Rangers' ground, before 12,000 spectators, to decide who should hold the cup for the ensuing year, and be- come the possessors of the gold medals. Runcorn played their full strength, but the Oldhamers were short of Martin (suspended) and S. Lees (suffering from an in- jury). Oldham won the toss and decided to play from the Broughton end. J. Faulkner set the ball in motion, but the kick was charged down by Bonsor, who sent the oval into the hands of the kicker. The last named wen; towards his opponents' lines, and when within measur- able distance passed to his brother, who easily scored within a few seconds of the start. Its value, however, was not increased by Barton. This early reverse had a very desultory effect on the Oldham men. Houghton and Hurst exchanged kicks, and Varley sent the ball dead with a big punt. On resuming, the Oldhamers pressed, but Warder cleared his lines manfully. Good work by Myers saw the scene of play at centre, a free kick to the Cheshire men ha;ing the effect of making their opponents adopt defensive tactics. The ball quickly travelled to the other end, however, and free kicks followed to both sides. The Runcorn team then had to defend for a time, but W. Faulkner ran splendidly and passed to J. Faulkner, the latter handing to one of the other for- wards, who was tackled on the line. The forwards gradually relieved, but only for a short time, as J. Faulkner again gave the Oldhamers a fright with a smart dribble, Eagland dropping on the leather just in time. Varlev at length gained possession from a scrum- mage, and with a long punt sent play into neutral ter- ritory. A free kick, taken by the same player, found touch within a few yards of the Runcorn line. W. Faulkner brought relief with a timely punt, but the game was still fought inside the Runcorn 25 yards flag. Hurst was cheered for go )d play, but the oval went into the hands of Houghton, who replied with interest. A free kick then fell to the lot of Oldham, and Eagland took the place from near the centre line, and only just missed landing a goal by inches. After the drop-out, Warder ran to the other end, and passed to Myers, the latter getting within measurable distance before he was pulled down. Runcorn were awarded a free kick oppo- site the posts, and the ill-luck of Oldham still continued, as Hughes increased the Runcorn lead by placing a lovely goal. Oldham again put on pressure after the ball had once been put into motion, hut the Runcorn tackling was superb, and they did not let their opponents gain any advantage whatever. Scrummages ensued within a few yards of the Runcorn strong- hold, but Pendlehury ran out and punted. Hurst replying only poorly. Not to be denied, the Old- hamers returned. Houghton saving magnificently. The heshire forwards eventually found their way to mid- field). Varley sending into touch inside the Runcorn half. When the whistle blew for half time the Runcorn team led by a goal and a try (6 points) to nil. Varley resumed, and the return kick sent the oval back to the centre. nigh kicking between Hurst and J. Faulkner took place, ending in favour of the latter. W Faulkner again almost brought the downfall of the Oldhamers from a penalty kick, hut the oval went wide. Varley then sent the ball to the other side of the ground, Hurst and M'Cutcheon were almost in, relief being eventually brought to the Runcorners by a touch down. At the Oldham end. Eagland was tackled with the ball through dallying too long, the Runcorn forwards following up in lively fashion. Try as they would the Oldhamers could not get out of their own half. Lees made a mark, but did not gain any ground thereby. The representatives of the cotton manufacturing town now played like a beaten team, and Eagland was forced to kick dead. From the drop-iut Oldham went away with a dash, but the opposing defence was too safe to be broken through. By gradual stages the half-way line was reached. Eagland deserved credit for a smart run and punt, and Varley almost dropped a goal, the ball dropping short. This did them little good, however, as iinuehton sent the leather flying into touch at centre. MiCutclseon at last got a pass from Lees, and handing the ball to Hurst that player crossed at the corner amid great cheering, which was renewed when Eagland placed a goal. The interest of the spec- tators was now aroused to a very high pitch. The return to Faulkner's kick was charged down, and the ball found a resting place near the Oldham line. Centre was gradually reached, and Oldham missed another chance through Etchells making a bad pass when there was no- body to pass. When the whistle blew Runcorn left the field possessors of the cup and medals. FINAL SCORE:

C. T. Runcorn Oldham *Penalty . _ . _ • _.. _ . .

After the match the President of the Lancashire Divi- sion of the Northern Union (Mr. J. E. Warren) pre- sented the cup and medals to the winners. He remarked that the match just ended was a thing to be remembered by the Northern Union. Suggestions had been made by the Lancashire County Committee as to the advisability of the Northern Unionists returning to their old love,but there was little, if any, liklihood of that coming to pass. When the clubs which now composed the Northern Union asked for reforms which they considered neces- sary, they were refused, and so they started a union of their own. Seeing what a success the Northern Union had achieved, they would not bow to the ruling of the Rugby Union. (Cheers.) Mr. Warren then presented the cup and medals to Mr. Faulkner, on behalf of the Runcorn team, amidst prolonged cheering. Mr. Lang- shaw replied on behalf of the Runcorn club.

===== 0002601_18960502_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEWS AT RUNCORN.

For the last three weeks nothing has been more the subject of speculation in Runcorn than the possibility of the local 15 being able to secure the Lancashire Cup and Medals,and as week after week Runcorn have emerged from engagements successfully the excitement has be- come more intense. On Saturday last it only needed a defeat of Oldham to put the trophy in Runcorn hands without further contest, but the Oldham team managed to again place itself on level terms with Runcorn. On Wednesday afternoon the day on which the two teams were to meet with the deciding tie a well laden special train left Runcorn for Broughton. Great anxiety waa felt in Runcorn as to the result of the match, but though the game at Broughton was started at 6.15 it was con- siderably after seven o'clock before the half time result of six points Runcorn,to Oldham nil came to hand. The exhibition of this to a crowd of at least 700 people ! gave the liveliest satiif action. The final result came to hand about 8.20 and was the signal for an extraordinary outburst of enthusiasm, at least 3,000 people were cheering, shouting, and throwing up their hats, while gangs of lads were singing, doggrel rhy- mes chiefly extolling the virtues of the "linnets." From nine to ten the crowd began to dribble towards the station where preparations were being made to celebrate the incoming of the cup in a manner befitting the occa- sion. Two four-in-hands were waiting for the train,and as the train steamed across the bridge another enthusias- tic demonstration took place. The four-in-hand, witb the captain and the cup conspicuously displayed on the front seat,and the successful team,were driven the long- est way back to the headquarters, and when the excite- ment was almost over a heavy shewer of rain quietly, but effectively cleared the people from the streets. The procession passed on amidst much excitement through High-A., Regent-st., Church-st., and Bridge-st., finishing in one triumphant shout as the team entered their headquarters—Wilson's Hotel. As the players en- tered there was a general squeeze by the public into the assembly room. Mr. Speakman, the president, on gett- ing into the chair received a wild ovation. He said they would agree with him that the occasion was one of the proudest days in the history of Rwicorn football. He assured those present that he hadonever seen a club work from beginning to end as the Runcorn lads had done that day. (Applause.) The team had played 42 matches and had made heavy sacrifics, and they deserved a 1 the honour that could possibly be given them for having brought that handsome trophy home. That day they had shown the whole of Lancashire that they knew how to play football (ap- plause), and he could tell them something more. The Runcorn players were Runcorn men. (Loud cheers.) He was not going to say mile word about any other club, but their team had brought that cup to Runcorn as amateurs. (Loud applause.) It was a proud day ler them, and he was sure that the Runcorn club in their success had the good wishes of the whole of the p.ople in this part of the League, because they were amateurs and not professionals. At, this stage of the proceedings cheers were called for W. Faulkner, the captain, and were given with cordia- lity. The cup was filled to the brim with champagne, and Mr. Speakman had the first drink from the new trophy proposing at the same time the health of the teara and their gallant captain. The cup was then handed round, and conviviality reigned supreme. Healths were drunk and toasts were proposed until the hour prescribed by law forced the proceedings to a termination. DESCRIPTION OF THE cur.

The cup itself,in which the admiration chiefly centres, is not only valuable by virtue of the honour attached to the winning of it, but it also possesses a considerable intrinsic value. Standing a considerable height,it repre- sents a mass of solid silver weighing just over 145 beautifully proportioned,and is a handsome specimen of the silversmith's art. On one side is the inscriptio " Lancashire Sniior Competition. Established 1895 6." On the opposite side is an artistically engraved represen- tation of a smart tackling movement on the football field. The whole is surmounted by a running figure, so well carved and poised as to be almost lifelike. It is now- on view in the premises of the general captain, Mr. W. Faulkner, and has been seen by a great number of foot- ball enthusiasts. The medals,of hich 15 are presented by the Northern Union, are of solid gold, and are esti- mated to be worth about £5 each.
