THE EXAMINER SATURDAY, MAY 2,149 G.

THE QUESTION OF BAD TRADE AT RUNCORN.

MYSTERIOUS DEATH OF AN EATtLES- TOWN MAN.

THE INQUEST.

At the Warrington Workhouse on Wednesday morn- ing, Mr. Brighouse, coro or, conducted an inquiry rela- tive to the death of David S ephens, 44 years of age, a labourer,for many years enaploycd at the Sankey Sugar Works, Earlestown, and who died at the Warrington Infirmary at ten o'clock on Monday night. Mr. James Titchett was the foreman of the jury. Ann Stephens said the deceased was her husband. He was 44 years of age last birthday, and was a labourer. He lived at 17, London-road, near the Sankey Sugar Works, Earlestown. He had been a teetotaller for four years and a half, but " broke out " at Easter, and the consequence was that he lost his employment. Since that time her husband had been nearly mad. On Wednesday last week he came home sober,and went I o bed about nine o'clock, and at 2.30 the next morning her son, who had been asleep on the sofa in the kitchen downstairs,called to her. She went downstairs, and found her husband lying on his back, and his head resting on the hearth- stone. He was "fair stiff." He was picked up, p:aced on a sofa, and she and her son subsequently assisted him to bed. A doctor was sent for, and Dr. Valentine was soon in attendance. Deceased was able to walk up- stairs with their assistance. She asked her husband why he came downstairs, and he replied that he " thought some one was driving wire into his forehead."— The Coroner: There has been a rumour that there was a quarrel in the house. Is that so ?--The witness re- plied in the negative.—ln answer to the Coroner, she said that the injuries to her head (which was in band- ages) had been caused by a blow from her husband with a chair. The deceased had told her that he became " dizzy" and fell downstairs.

The-Coroner,at this stage of the proceedings,requested the other witnesses to leave the room, and when they had done so, Michael Connor, step-son of the deceased, said that Margaret and David Stephens slept in the same room as their father and mother. He usually slept with his brother, but on the night when his father came home drunk he was downstairs in the kitchen. FP3 was awakened about half-past two by hearing a heavy fall. He found his father on the hearthstone with bis head across the fender. His father was drunk at the time ; he had no wound on his head th in except a small one behind the ear. He had never had any quarrel with his father, nor at any time had he struck him. They had all !lived happily until his father broke the pledge at Easter.

Dr. Braide said the deceased was admitted to the Workhouse Hospital, where he died on Monday night. He had made a post mortem examination of the body. There was externally a slight wound on the right ear. On opening the brain he found a lot of matter, between the bone and the membrane of the brain, chiefly on the right side, and pressing on the right lobe of the brain. This was probably of a fortnight's standing, because the brain was softening on the right side. On exainining the bone at the base of the scull a slight fracture was seen about one and a half inches long, round which inton- matory changes had taken place, and these were quite sufficient to account for the death of the deceased. Alexander Sullivan, 80, Legi-street, Earlestown, step- son of deceased, said that he saw his father well and hearty on Good Friday at Leigh and Bedford. He left him on Saturday night, and witness did not see him again until the Tuesday following. at E trlestown, when he had a number of cuts on his head. He told witness that "they had killed him." He then said when he got home his stepsons wanted him to go to bed without sup- per. When he would not, they got hold of him and " shoved "him upstairs. They then started falling out and the old woman got a smack with a chair. He (de- ceased) then ran downstairs, when someone came with a bottle or jug and gave him a hit on the head with it. On;Saturday night,when witness left deceased,the latter was quite sober. He had only had four glasses of port wine that afternoon.

The Coroner : He has been drinking port wine during the last four years,when you say he is teetotal ?—Yes, sir. I consider he is teetotal when he only drinks port wine and cider.

The Coroner: It is said that he was mad drunk when he got home P— Witness : Whether or not he got some drink on his way home I cannot say, but he was not drunk when he left me. _. .

Michael Connor was at this point recalled,and in reply to the Coroner he reiterated that his stenfather was mad drunk when he came home. He denied that any blows were struck at deceased, and also denied that the latter was hit with a jug.

The Coroner said that ha did not see what verdict should be returned except an open one. If anything further were discovered to be suspicious,the police could take the matter up.

After a consultation in private, the jury returned an open verdict, ia accordance with the coroner's advice.

STOCKTON HEATH.

===== 0002601_18960502_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
INTERESTING CONFERENCE.

On Monlay afternoon a conference was held in the Town Hall, Runcorn, to consider whether anything can be done to improve the trade of Runcorn. The meeting which was the outcome of a suggestion thrown out by Mr. Jones and was largely attended, amongst those ore- sent being Sir. John T. Brunner, Bart., M-P., the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Messrs. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., J. W. Lightbura J.P., J. Pritchard, H. J. Grice. G. H. Kirk- ham, J. Horsfield, T. Dan, W. Handley, D. Bisbrown, J. Hewitt, M. Sweetman, J. R. Firth, H. Branch, G. Summerfteld, E. Tuckey, B. Knivaon, J. Stubbs, W. H. Chadwick, R. Abel, W. Upton, E. Marshall (secretary), and F. A. L ike, (law clerk).—ln opening the meeting, Mr. Lightburn staced that Mr. Jones who originated the movement had been taken so ill that he could not possibly attend and had not been able to elaborate his scheme. They were therefore met to ascertain the opin- ions of gentlemen interested in different industries to see what would be the best course to adopt. In the first place they wanted a chairman, and they would be glad to see Sir. J. T. Brunner take that position and thus give the movement an impetus it would not otherwise possess. The hon. gentl,:man's assistance would do a great deal of good to a community with the need of help, and if he acceded to their wishes he would do an act which would commend itself to the people pf Runcorn. He would propos that Sir J. T. Brunner be chairman of the corn. mittee.

Sir J. T. Branner, said he would greatly prefer to see them give one of their townsmen the dignity of the chair- manship. He was at all times as far as his abilities allowed rum ready to place his services at their disposal. He desired that one who was always on the spot, and who was willing t: devote his consideration to the matters described as very important should have the honour of tne position, whilst he undertook the re- sponsibility of the work. _

- Mr. Ligfitburn said that every gentleman in the town was willing to surrender that honour if Sir John would accept the position.

The Rev. Dr. Gastaldi had great pleasure in seconding Mr. Lightburn's motion. _ .

Mr. Lightburn thought that if Sir John would under- take the chairmanship, Mr. Speakman would accept the office of vice cl4airman, and they then would have two commercial gentlemen at the head of the movement. Sir J. Brunner wa3 very sorry he could not see his way to fall in with the wishes expressed by the gentlemen present. If Mr. Speakman was going to do the work he should be chairman. As far as his apilities and time gave him opportunities to work for the prosperity of Runcorn he would do so.—Suhsaquently on the motion of Mr. Lighthurn, seconded by Mr. Dean, Mr. Speakman was unanimously elected to the chair,and Mr. Jones was elected vice chairman.

The Chairman said they in Runcorn found themselves in a poor condition since the Ship Canal undertak- ing was completed. The men •engaged on the under- taking had left the town, and the town had felt their departure. The other industries in the neighbour- hood were not by any means in the condition they would like to see them, and any wise suggestions made would be of advantage to the district. He would be glad of any expression of opinion. Mr. Chadwick stated that before Sir John Brunner's arrival he had been asking whether the town was pre- pared to offer any advantages to new industries coming in their midst. When he asked the question Mr. Grice mentioned the deep docks the W arrington Corporation contemplated providing. If Runcorn could do something similar they might succeed in doing something to en- hance the trade of the town and port. Mr. Lightburn thought it was impossible to answer that question at the meeting. The Council could not in his opinion do anything to bbe charged upon the rates and the committee would have to bear their own ex- penses. There were other matters also demanding con- sideration. They might make some arrangements for preferential rates to large concerns. To this, objections would doubtless present themselves tor if they offered lower rates to new concerns Ult old ones would natur- ally expect a proportionate reduction. The offering of special advantages would largely tend to bring new in- dustries amongst them.

Mr. Handley- pointed out that chemical works were already being erected at Weston Point and if rumour spoke correctly they would develope and provide work for a large number of hands. He thought the first thing they had to find out was what special trade was the town adapted to and how they could obtain those trades. He could not shut his eyes to the fact that the inception of large industries would take a long time and consider- able delay would occur before they had important re- sults. These depended upon certain conditions—the rates, water supply, land, water oarriage, and railway sidings which were big questions. So far as rates were concerned he could throw a little light upon it by what had been done. The Ship Canal Co. were rated on a comparatively low assessment. Originally this was put at £l,boo as far as this district was concerned, and he thought it now stood at £9OO or £9lO, because the committee having considered the cir- cumstances, felt they ought to do what they could to assist them. lie alluded to what he said a few days ago, when the appointment of a committee was under consideration. He suggests' the establishment of glue works seeing the material was made from the offal of tanneries. They had snap works but there were many other things which came from it such as dry soap which would employ a lot of female labour. Mr. Handley also gave other illustrations of trade to which the town of Runcorn was adapted.

Sr John Brunner stated that undoubtedly Mr. Light- burn was right in advising that the whole subject should be considered by a small committee. It was necessary in the first instance if they wanted to invite trade that they should possess certain information. They should ascertain what land was available, who were the owner& to be approached,so that the price for purchase or rental could be obtained. Mr. Handley had said that there were certain buildings availabla. If these were capable of being converted into manufactories, the committee shonld be acquainted with them. Mr. Lightburn had dealt, with an ability which surprised him, with the legal aspect of the ques ion. He suggested that they should have the presence of Mr. Lake, but this was al- together unnecessary, seeing the information possessed by the Chairman of the Council. He was perfectly ignor- ant as to their powers to reduce their rates for intending manufacturers, but he was perfectly well aware that small communities in the United States were able to offer inducements to capitalists to settle in the district, by agreeing that no local rates should be charged for two, five Cr ten years. How far they could go in that direction under their mode of local government he confessed himself in ignorance, but it would be the duty of the committee to obtain know- ledge upon that subject. It had been suggested that the local authority might obtain certain powers, and there again it would be valuable if the sub-committee informed itself, and made a careful report to the gemeral commit- tee. They had heard, rather indefinitely, that the Cor- poration of Warrington had undertaken the construction of decks. If that were so, it would be wise for them to ascertain the circumstances under which they were tak- ing action. At that meeting it was quite expected that the proceedings would be preliminary in character, and they would discuss as to what steps should be taken to ensure the prosperity of the town in the future. He was inclined to think that the appointment of a sub-committee to consider the suggestions made that day was as far as they could go. Wherever they went they saw pretty pictures at the railway stations. &c., setting forth the beauties of various watering places, and he understood the adver- tisements were paid for by the local Corporations. It might be that an occasional modest advertisement an- nouncing the fact that premises were to be had in such a situation, with certain advantages, at a low mita' for a few years, if inserted in a trade journal, might brine' in the long run many hundreds of pounds into the pockets of the working people of the town. He should be cordially interested in perusing the report, when published ,and he would use his best endeavours to make it useful for the future welfare of the town. (Applause.) On the proposition of Mr. Lightburn, it was resolved that the sub-committes should consist of the Chairman and Vice-Chairman, Messrs. Pritchard, Kniveton, Grice, Handley, Chadwick, Taylor, Imison, Pope, Lightburn, Kirkham, Brancli,and F. J. N,)rman. The Chairman said it would be for the general com- mittee to send suggestions to the sub-committee so that they might go forward. He hoped the work would be conducted so that it would bear valuable results.

PRESENTATION AT NEWTON.

On Friday evenizig Mr. Samuel Barrett, was the recipient of a handsome timepiece and pencil case, on the occasion of his severing his connection with the firm of Messrs. McCorquodale and Co., of Newton, in whose service he has occupied the posi- tion ot Chief Stationer for several years. Mr. Wil- liam Speakman who made the presentation in the presence of the subscribers, said they had met to- gether to show their appreciation of the long service and personal worth of Mr. Barrett, by offering to him that present. as a memento of his Newton friends, and a tribute of the high esteem and regard they entertained towards him. They were very sorry to lose him, and were surprised when, they learnt that he had accepted an appointment under another firm, but they offered him their hearty con- gratulations and best wishes for his future welfare. Messrs. Adams, Crouchley and Dick spoke in simi- lar terms, and in reply, Mr. Barrett, said he was ex- ceedingly obliged to them for the many kind expressions he had heard. It was painful for him to break from his old associations, but he did not think he left behind him a single enemy. The time- piece bore the following inscription "Presented to Mr. Samuel Barrett, as a token of their regret and esteem of his fellow etnployes of of McCorquodale and Co., Limited, Newton-le-Willows, April 24th, 1896,"

A-CROFT HOTEL PROPRIETOR AND HIS UNSTAISIPED GLASSES.

At Newton Petty Sessions on Saturday morning, Mr. T. J. Gillespie (chairman) and Colonel Wood were en- gaged for over two hours in hearing a case in which Henry Walker, landlord of the General Eliot Hotel,Croft, was charged at the instance of Mr. Shepherd. inspector of weights and measures, with having on the 3rd April, had in use for trade three unstamped glasses.—Mr. Stuart, of Newton, prosecuted, and Mr. Knowles, of Widnes, defended.—ln stating the case, Mr. Stuart in- formed the Bench that Mr. Shepherd went, in company with P.C. Callaghan, to the General Eliot Hotel on Good Friday. They proceeded to a bar in one of the rooms and asked for half-a-pint of beer and a hop ale. This was supplied in the glasses produced,which were unstamped. The glasses were taken from a number of others by the defendant's men which showed that they were in use for trade. Shepherd took possession of three glasses in which beer had beet' supplied, and none of them were stamped as required by Act of Parliament.—lnenector Shepherd corroborated Mr. Stuart's statement. There were about 200 people in the room in which he was served with the beer in unstamped glasses. Witness asked the man who appeared to be in charge, how it was he was supplyin2 the beer in unstamped glasses. He replied that it was a mistake. Witness asked him for his name, but he re- fused to give it,and witness then told him that he should take the three glasses produced. Witness afterwards saw the manager and asked him how it was he was sup- plying half pints of beer in unstamped glasses. Be realied that it was a mistake and was ow- ing to them being so busy. On locking at the smallest glass preduced,defeniant said that was a spirit glass and not a beer glass. Witness told him that glasses of a similar description were being used for supplying beer.—Cross-examined, witness said he did not think it necessary to take the glasses with the beer in to the landlord. Defendant did not tell him that there were SO dozen stamped half-pint glasses in the house.-- Re-examined by Mr. Stuart.witnese said one glass when filled contained one oz., seven drachms less than the re- quired quantity. If all the beer were served in glasses that size four barrels would go as far as five—P.C. Callaghan gave corroborative evidence. In cross-ex- amination,he said he saw three unstamped glasses at the bar. There was a ereat number of unstamped glasses in use. There would be between two hundred and three hundred people in the room that witness and the in- spsctor went in. For the defence, Mr. Knowles said Good Friday was a day on which a great number of people resorted to Craft. The General Eliot Hotel was a place where a large business was done, and every precaution was taken to see that the law was observed. An extra staff of policeman was engaged by the proprietor, and the visitors were not encouraged to drink. The defendant was an experienced man in his business. He had always been used tea large trade and was very practical. He had been at the General Eliot 13otel for about sixteen months. On Good Friday morning he got his assistants together and told them that they were not to serve beer except in stamped half-pint measures. There was a rack under the beer-pumps on which the stamped glasses were placed ready to hand. and the other glasses were placed on a shelf behind the assistants. It had been clearly laid down tat if a servant acted in disobedience, and disregarded the positive orders given, the landlord was not liable,and Mr. Knowles cited an appeal case in which this had been held. Proceeding to speak of the glasses in use at the hotel, Mr. Knowles said one of the glasses produced did not belong to the hotel. There were none others like it in the place. and ' how it got there no one could tell. The other two glasses were known as table glasses, and were used only at dinner pude,. It was unusual to have stamped glasses on the dinner table. There wore 4 dz. of these glasses at the hotel, and they were kept on a shelf behind the waiters. The defendant was unable to account for some of these glasses being brought into use as there were plenty of stamped ones. Mr. Stuart said he wished to point out that all the glasses in the house should have been stamped under the Weights and Measures Act. He contended that the ease quoted—Newman and Jones—did not fit on all fours with the present case. Under the general principle of the Licensing Act it was laid down that the landlord was responsible for the acts of his servants. Henry- Walker, proprietor of the General Eliot Hotel, Croft, said he had been in the licensing trade for sixteen I years. He corroborated his solicitor'e statement with regard to the glasses.—Cross-examined, witness said the waiters would be discharged if they did not obey his orders.—Walter Raynor, Eciward Falby, Henry Shaw, mid Walter Hatton, assistants for the defendant on the date named, deposed to receiving instruct ens from their employer as to serving beer in stamped glasses. The Chairman said they had given the case careful consideration, and they were under the impression that orders were given by the defendant. The case would be di,qmissed.

Mr. Stuart said there were two other charges against the defendant, viz., for using unjust measures and also for a breach of the licensing act. The point raised in the Newman and Jones case could not 'amply to the charge of unjust measures.—Mr. Knowles contended that all the cases chould stand or fall together.—The magistrates upheld this view, and the cases were there- ore not proceeded with.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

MONDAY.

At the commencemeat of business in the House of Commons on Monday the Speaker ruled out of order three proposed instructions to the Committee on the London and North-Western Railway Bill directing the Committee to insert provi ions compelling the Company to run third-class carriages on the mail trains between Ruston and Holyhead at the usual rate per mile. Mr. CIIAMBSRIAIN, in reply to Mr. Labouchere.said he believed the Chartered Company of South Africa had about half a million in cash. He did not propoie to obtain security from the Company for the repayment of the cost of the suppression of the Matabele rising. Replying to Sir E. A. Bartlett and Mr. Buxton, Mr. Chamberlain said the Government received on Saturday, by telegraph, the substance of President Kruger's reply to their invitation to him to visit England. The Presi- dent said it appeared to him to be the wiser course not to press the question of his proceeding to England any further at present, but to leave it an open matter, more especially in view of the approaching session of the volksraad. Under these circumstances the Government had reluctantly withdrawn their invitation to the Presi- dent to come to England, and they had asked that Sir Graham Bower should come to England as soon as pos- in order that the Government might confer with him as to any further negotiations which might be decided upon. _ _

Sir W. HARCOURT asked whether arrangements had been made with the Government of the United States for the settlement by arbitration of the matter ;n dispute re- specting the boundary of Venezuela. Mr. A. J. Balfour replied that the last communication from the United States Government, which was received on Friday last, dealt both with the general question of arbitration and also with the special question connected with Venez For the moment that was all the information he could OVA. Mr. BALFOUR afterwards moved a resolution directing that for the remainder of the session the House should meet at three o'clock on Tuesdays ; that Government business should have priority over all other Bills and notices of motion on every day for which it was ap- pointed ; and that the provisions of Standing Order No. 56 should be extended to every day of the week. He explained that the Government intended to pass the Agricultural Rating Bill and the Education Bill " under all circumstances," and that it dependea upon the length of time the Irish members wished to spend upon the Irish Land Bill whether that was passed or not. After some debate the original res9lotion was then carried by 268 votes to 124. Mr. CHAPLIN moved the second reading of the Agricul- tural Rating Bill. Sir 11. H. Fowler moved as an amend- ment that in the opinion of the House it would be in- expedient and unjust that relief granted from Imperial taxation to rateable property should be restricted to one class only of such property. The debate was ad- journed. TUESDAY.

In the House of Commons on 'Tuesday Mr. Curzon told Mr. Bowles that claims for the unlawful seizure of British sealing vessels in the Behring Sea were under dis- cuQsion with the Russian Government.

Mr. VICTOR CAYRNDISR asked whether any informa- tion had been received as to the recent trials at Pretoria. Mr. Chamberlain replied that he had received a private telegram to the effect that five leaders of the Reform Committee had been sentenced to death. Upon that in- formation the Government telegraphed to Sir H. Robin- son the following message for communication to Presi- desnt Kruger :—" Her Majesty's Government have just learned that sentence of death has been passed on the chief leaders of the Reform Corimittee. They can feel no doubt that your Honour will commute that sentence, and they have assured Parliament that it is their con- viction that that is your Honour's intention." In reply to a further question, Mr. Chamberlain said four of the leaders who had been condemned were British subjects, and the fifth was an American. The debate was resumed on the motion for the second reading of the Agricultural Land Rating Bill, and the amendment moved by Sir H. Fowler, to the effect that the House, while recvnising, the desirableness of read- justing the burdens of local taxation, was of opinion that it was inexpedient and unjust that the relief granted from Imperial taxation to rateable property should be restricted to one class only of such property. The de- bate had not concluded when the House adjourned.

WEDNESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Wednesday the discus- sion on Sir H. H. Fowler's amendment to the motion for the second reading of the Agricultural Land Rating Bill was resumed by Mr. T. SHAW. Mr. G. WHITHLEY urged that there should be a time limit to the Bill. He op- posed the measure on the ground of the exceedingly cruel and grevious injustice that weuld be done to the borough and urban districts. He contended that Lan- cashire was entirely opposed to the Bill. Mr. LAnou- MERE said he could not support the amendment, as it seemed t, imply that under certain conditions Parlia- ment might be inclined to give a further subvention to relieve local burdens. He objected to all subventions from the Imperial Treasury. In the course of further discussion Colonel MELLOR as another Lancashire mem- ber, denied that Mr. Whiteley's speech represented the feeling of that country, tut agreed that a time limit should be introduced in the Bill. The debate stood ad- ourned at half-past five o'clock.

CHARGE AGAINST A STOCKTON HEATH BUILDER.

Before a full bench of magistrates at Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday,John Pemberton, builder, Stockton Heath,was charged that on March 13th. at Appleton, he unlawfully contravened byelaw 91, of the Runcorn Rural District Council, with having neglected to deliver com- plete plans of buildings proposed to be erected, as re- quired by such bvelaw.—Mr. Arthur Browne prosecuted on behalf of the Runcorn Council,and Mr. T. Swift, bar- rister,instructed by Mr. Wallhead,solicitor, Warrington, defended.

Mr. Browne said the defendant was prosecuted for an offence against the byelaws made by the Runcorn Rural Council which said that every person wh ) intended to erect buildings should give notice which should be de- livered in writing and which should be delivered at the office of the clerk of the authority,and should give com- plete plans and specifications of such proposed buildings. In the present case, the defendant purchased some land belonging to Pike's trustees. Before he obtained posses- ' sion of the land, a block plan was submitted by the de- fendant in October 1892. showing how it was intended the passages should be laid out. The plan was passed in the ordinary way in 1892, showing the requisite street.' and passages. In June 1893, the defendant submitted plans for fifty houses to the Sanitary Authority,and they were refused- on account of not allowing for a certain passage and certain street. Mr. Farrington sent notice of the resolution of the committee,stating that in conse- quence of the plans not allowing a passage and street the committee at their meeting in Aug. 1893,had rejected them. Mr. Farrington had net kept a copy of the notice but his postage book showed that he posted a letter to Mr. John Pemberton on the 31st August. That Mr. Pemberton knew the plans were rejected was shown by the fact that he sent in another plan which showed 17 houses only upon it. This plan did not show a pass- age leading opposite to St. Thomas' Church, and the Chairman of the committee struck his •pen through that part of the plan as being disapproved of. A fresh plan was requisite before any building on that part could he proceeded with, and this was 'where the offence com- plained of had taken place. A lot of correspondence had ensued on the matter,and in one letter the defendant admitted that he was aware that the plan he submitted had not been approved of. A new act had come into force, and Mr. Farrington had to make way for a new surveyor in the person of Mr. Swinton. The defendant did not make any attempt to build until a few months eon, knowing perfectly well that the plans had not been passed. That was when Mr. Farrington was not the surveyor and the fresh one did not know what had taken place in the matter. The defendant had got part way up with the houses, but he had done it •with his eyes open. He had written to say that his bricksetters were waiting to go on with the work, but yet he knew that his plans had never been passed. He had set the auth- ority at defiance in going on with the building, and he (Mr. Browne) contended that it was a procedure which should be stopped with a stroll.' hand. Mr. James Farrington, inspector of nuisances for the Runcorn Rural District Council and formerly surveyor, corroborated Mr. Brown's statement.—ln cross-exam- ination, witness admitted that the defendant's architect sent in plans in June, 1893. He did not write telling defendant that the plans bad been refused until August.

Mr. A. Swinton, surveyor, said he was appointed to that position under the Rural Council in 1895. The defendant commenced building over a passage on the 13th March last as shown in the original plans. He (Mr. Swinton) was not aware of a letter which was written by the Chairman of the Appleton Parochial Committee stat- ing that the committee were of the opinion that the road past Stockton Heath Church, should certainly be made, and that Mr.Pemberton and Mr. Wallington should make arrangements with the lessor of the land since the road was a necessity for the convenience of the residents for whom the houses were beieg constructed. Witness was not aware that the defendant had been trying to get the road. Witness admitted that he had told defen- dant to postpone building operations until he (witness) had tried to make some arrangements about the road. • Mr. Browne pointed out that the parochial committee was not the acting authority. Their recommendations had to be confirmed by .the Sanitary Authority.

For the defence, Mr. Swift said he submitted with the greatest confidence that the defendant was entitled to have the summons dismissed. Defendant was not sum- moned for commencing to build when plans had been rejected. They were charged with having neglected to deliver crannlete plans. The defendant, as was admitted, did not send in plans for 50 houses, and upon that plan 17 houses had already been erected. The plans had been in the possession of the prosecutors for about two years. Mr. Farrington admitted that they were deposited in June, 1893, and were submitted to the committee in August of the same year. It did not matter one iota in the present case whether the plans were rejected or ap- proved of. Every tittle of evidence which had been given went to show that defendant did not neglect to deliver complete plans. The minutes of the authority showed that the plans were submitted, and stated that "owing to the houses not being laid out in accordance with the approved plans of new streets they were re- jected." Not a single word of complaint was made about the plans not being complete," or that they were contrary to the hyelaws. He further wished to point out that it was laid down in the Public Eiealth Act, that within one month from the plans being deposited,thesur- veyor or clerk should signify to the sender in writing the authority's approval or disapproval of the intended work. Be (Mr. Swift) contended that his client did not receive such a communication within the specified period. The defendant had been willing to postpone building some of the houses until an arrangement had been made about the road. Mr. Swinton wrote stating that he would endeavour to make such an arrangement in January last, but he had done nothing in the matter up to the present. The disapproval of the plans was of am avail to the prosecution-if the defendant could prove that he had not disobeyed any by-law. Mr. Silcock, surveyor and architect,said he submitted plans for Mr. Pemberton to the Authority in June. 1893. They were prepared in accordance with the On being cross-examined with regard to depositing plans on behalf of Pike's trustees, Mr. Swift told the witness not to answer, contending that it had nothing to do with the present case.

Mr. Browne speaking on a point of law, stated that Mr. Swift's contention that the defendant had deposited " complete" plans was a reductio absurdum. The plan for the buildings which were now in course of erection had been rejected. The Bench retired ti consider their decision and on their return the Chairman said they agreed that there was proof that complete plans were deposited but that no notice of objection to them was delivered in time by the officers of the authority. The case would therefore be dismissed. They would make no order as regarded costs.

Tan Crrunar INSTITIITP.—The annual general meeting of the members of the above was held in the Church Room on Thursday evening last week, under the presid- ency of the Rev. W. S. Hulme. There was a very good attendance. The Hon. Secretary explained the new rules for the working of the room next season which had been drawn up by the trustees of the room, and the same were adopted by the general body of members. The trea- surer's accounts were presented by Mr. Edwin Bradahaw, who explained that starting with an adverse balance last year the balance of this year was very favour- able, and there was sufficient in hand to start the coming season with. The amount received from the Bazaar Committee had been spent on making the room eo.nplete and putting same in good order, and there still was a small balance in hand en that ac- count. The question of the disposal of the balance in hand which belonged under the old rules, to the Church Institute, was raised, and it was unanimonaly decided that same be handed over to the managers for the general working expenses of the room in the future. A suggestion was made that it was advisable to purchase a piano for the room and the matter was left to the managers, who have had the matter before them on previous occasions.—rh3 Chairman proposed a vote of thanks to the Hon. Secretary, Hon. Treasurer, and the committeeopeaking of their hard work and the readiness with which they carried out same, in very eulogistic terms,also mentioning that he could not speak too highly of them for the way in which they had always helped him in his work in the parish, and concluding by asking the members to stand by and help them in their work ; and wishing the room all the StnCPSR and prosperity it deserved. This was seconded and carried with acclamation—The Hon. Secrefary,on behalf of the committee,thanked the Chairman for his remarks and begged to pronose in return a hearty vote of thanks to him for the way in which he had helped the committee in their arduous work, and cm this being seconded it was carried amid applause. Votes of thanks were also accorded to Mr. F. Broady for auditing the accounts and to the members for their assistance during the past year. The meeting then discussed the anneal picnic:, and after some discussion it was resolved that the " Dukeries " should ha visited Ulla year. The Hon. Secretary t:ave particulars of the cost, &c. The date is as before, Warrington Walking Di • , and Mr. F. Timperlev has again kindly consented to receive the subscriptions in instalments from those who wish to buy their tickets in this way. Under the new rules the subscription for the next season for membership of the room is to be one shilling per annum,and every male over eighteen years of age in the parish who wishes to use the room habitually,is expected to pay this fee. The managers are not empowered to let the room to any body or organisation of males over that age,on more than two occasions in any one year. In other respects the rules are much the same as before,and the mem, ership and management will go on as before. It is hoped that this redueticn in the subscription (now a mere bagatelle) will be the means of a large increase in the membership and more frequent use of the room, though in this latter respect,the church room has during the season just closed been perhaps more often used than any similar room in the district.
